END OF PART 35 — v1.12 / v8.4 ADDITIONS: TRIANGLE-FIRST GEOMETRY (TRIS)
The "everything is a triangle" mental model is now first-class in the
LBL spec: 5 documented methods, 6-item pre-emission checklist, 1
new topbar button, 1 new validator, 5 renderer-side helpers on
`window.lblSpec.helpers`. Layers on top of PART 32's existing
`inspectModelTriangles` (extended, not replaced) and PART 30's
rigging pipeline (reinforced, not replaced). Nothing in this PART
removes or weakens any rule from PARTs 1-34.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 36 — v1.13 / v8.5 ADDITIONS: MULTI-FILE DROP + MODEL + RIG PAIRING
The renderer now accepts a FileList (or a multi-select file picker, or
a multi-file drag-and-drop) instead of only ever processing the first
file. The new behavior is opt-in by virtue of the user dropping more
than one file at a time — every single-file drop is unchanged. This
PART layers on top of PART 1 (single-file factory output contract),
PART 2 (types), PART 4 (imports), PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers
+ validators), PART 34 (pre-bundled rig helpers), and PART 35
(triangle-first geometry). The renderer gains:
  - `multiple` attribute on the 4 file inputs (file-input, file3d-input,
    dz-input, mob-dz-input) so the browser's file picker accepts a batch
  - a `lblRouteFiles(fileList)` dispatcher that buckets each File by
    extension / MIME and routes to the right loader
  - a `__lblLoaded3DModels[]` array that holds every uploaded 3D model
    in the current session (was a single `loaded3dGroup` before)
  - a new "rig" file type — a .ts/.mts/.cts file that doesn't define a
    `create*Model` factory but DOES expose a `RigGraph` data structure
================================================================================

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.1 — EXECUTIVE OVERVIEW: WHAT CHANGES FOR AI-AUTHORED FACTORIES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
NOTHING. A single model.ts file dropped by the user behaves exactly as
it always has. A single blueprint.json file is unchanged. A single
GLB/OBJ/STL is unchanged. A single image-texture drop is unchanged.

What changes is ONLY when the user drops more than one file at once:

  1. 3D models (.glb / .gltf / .fbx / .obj / .stl / .dae / .usdz)
     - The first one anchors at the origin (matches the old behavior).
     - Every subsequent one is APPENDED to the right of the previously
       loaded 3D models, with a 1.0-unit gap, and the camera is
       refit to the combined bounding box of all loaded models.
     - The scene tree shows one row per loaded model.
     - Each row is independently selectable; the gizmo / inspector /
       sub-mesh highlight all honor the per-model selection.

  2. Images (.png / .jpg / .jpeg / .gif / .webp / .svg / .bmp / .avif)
     - Each image is applied as a texture to the currently selected
       part (or spawns a new textured plane if nothing is selected).
     - All images in the batch are processed.

  3. Blueprints + rigs (.json / .js / .ts / .mts / .cts)
     - Loaded sequentially (one finishes before the next starts) so
       a rig file in the batch can attach to a model file that loaded
       earlier in the same drop.
     - The first blueprint replaces the scene (matches the old
       behavior). Subsequent blueprints trigger a toast: "Multiple
       blueprint files detected — drop them one at a time to load
       them sequentially." (Merging two full scenes is intentionally
       out of scope for the drop path; the editor paste path is the
       supported workflow for that.)
     - A .ts file that fails the regular `create*Model` factory check
       is automatically retried as a RIG (see 36.2). If it also
       fails as a rig, the user gets a clear error toast.

The 6-item pre-emission checklist in 35.6 and the 34.6 PART 34
checklist are unchanged. No new contract is imposed on .ts factories
— every model you wrote for v1.12 / v8.4 still loads, still exports,
still renders. The new behavior is purely additive on the dropper.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.2 — THE "RIG" FILE TYPE: 4 SUPPORTED EXPORT SHAPES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A .ts file is treated as a RIG (instead of a model) when:

  (a) it has no `create*Model` factory (so the regular pipeline throws
      "No create*Model() export found in <file>"), AND
  (b) it exposes a `RigGraph` data structure in one of these 4 shapes:

  SHAPE 1 — default export is a RigGraph OBJECT:
    ```ts
    export default {
      joints: [
        { id: 'hips',  parentId: null,     restPosition: [0, 1, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
        { id: 'spine', parentId: 'hips',  restPosition: [0, 0.6, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
        { id: 'head',  parentId: 'spine', restPosition: [0, 0.5, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] }
      ]
    };
    ```

  SHAPE 2 — default export is a FUNCTION that returns a RigGraph:
    ```ts
    export default function makeRig() {
      return { joints: [/* ... */] };
    }
    ```

  SHAPE 3 — named export `rigGraph` is a RigGraph:
    ```ts
    export const rigGraph = { joints: [/* ... */] };
    ```

  SHAPE 4 — named getter function returns a RigGraph:
    ```ts
    export function getRigGraph() { return { joints: [/* ... */] }; }
    // also accepted: createRigGraph, buildRigGraph, getRig, createRig
    ```

A RigGraph is `{ joints: Array<{ id: string, parentId: string|null,
restPosition: [number, number, number], restRotation: [number, number,
number, number] }> }`. The exact same shape the bundled
`three-rig-helpers` library consumes (see PART 34 for the full type
definitions and the validation rules).

When a rig is detected, the renderer:

  1. compiles the .ts file through the same TS pipeline as models
  2. dynamically imports the compiled module
  3. extracts the RigGraph via one of the 4 shapes above
  4. validates it with `three-rig-helpers.validateRigGraph(rigGraph)`
     (throws with a clear error message on duplicate joint ids,
     dangling parents, non-root trees, etc.)
  5. calls `three-rig-helpers.buildSkeleton(rigGraph)` to get
     `{ root: Bone, bones: Map, skeleton: Skeleton, restWorldPositions }`
  6. attaches `root` to the most recently loaded TS model group
  7. sets `userData.rigGraph` and `userData.skeletonSource = 'three-rig-helpers'`
     (the existing 🦴 Rig button + PART 34 spec checks then enable
     automatically — no separate "rig was applied" UI to wire)
  8. toasts: `✓ Rig applied — N joints on "<model name>"`

If no TS model is currently loaded, the rig is QUEUED in
`window.__lblPendingRigs` and a toast says
`"Rig "<file>" queued — drop a model .ts next and it will attach
automatically"`. The queue is drained in `addTsModelToScene()` right
after `CURRENT_TS_MODEL` is assigned, so a rig dropped first + a model
dropped second still ends up with the rig attached.

The renderer re-applies a rig to a model that already has one (drops
the old rig root before adding the new one), so re-dropping a rig
file replaces the previous skeleton — no duplicate Bones.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.3 — WHAT A RIG FILE MUST NOT DO
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The PART 4 single-file / no-relative-imports rules apply to rig files
the same as they apply to model files. Specifically:

  - Imports of `'three'` and `'three/addons/...'` are allowed (the
    import map resolves them). No relative imports to other files in
    the same drop.
  - No `fetch()` / `XMLHttpRequest` / `import()` of any URL outside
    the import map. The rig file must be self-contained.
  - No browser globals beyond what a model file is allowed to touch.
  - No `document.createElement` / direct DOM manipulation — the
    renderer's UI updates are driven by the userData markers set
    by the attach step, not by the rig file itself.

If the rig file does any of these, the user gets a clear error toast.
The spec does not enforce this with a separate linter — the renderer's
existing PART 4 validators handle it on the model side, and the
dynamic-import failure surfaces naturally on the rig side.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.4 — MINIMAL WORKED EXAMPLE: a model.ts + rig.ts pair
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
Drop these two files together (any order — model first or rig first
both work) and the rig attaches to the model automatically:

  // model.ts
  import * as THREE from 'three';

  export default function createCubeCharacter(opts = {}) {
    const g = new THREE.Group();
    g.name = 'CubeCharacter';

    const body = new THREE.Mesh(
      new THREE.BoxGeometry(1, 2, 0.6),
      new THREE.MeshStandardMaterial({ color: 0x88aaff, flatShading: true })
    );
    body.position.y = 1.0;
    body.castShadow = true; body.receiveShadow = true;
    g.add(body);

    const head = new THREE.Mesh(
      new THREE.BoxGeometry(0.7, 0.7, 0.7),
      new THREE.MeshStandardMaterial({ color: 0xffcc88, flatShading: true })
    );
    head.position.y = 2.35;
    head.castShadow = true; head.receiveShadow = true;
    g.add(head);

    g.userData.sculptRuntime = { tick: () => {} };
    return g;
  }

  // rig.ts — note: NO create*Model factory, so the dropper treats it
  //          as a rig and attaches it to the just-loaded model.
  export default {
    joints: [
      { id: 'hips', parentId: null,
        restPosition: [0, 1.0, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
      { id: 'spine', parentId: 'hips',
        restPosition: [0, 0.6, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] },
      { id: 'head', parentId: 'spine',
        restPosition: [0, 0.5, 0], restRotation: [0, 0, 0, 1] }
    ]
  };

What the user sees when they drop both:

  1. Toast: "Loading CubeCharacter.ts…"
  2. Model appears, centered on the origin, auto-camera-fit.
  3. Toast: "✓ Rig applied — 3 joints on CubeCharacter"
  4. The 🦴 Rig button (PART 34) becomes enabled — clicking it
     lists the 3-joint hierarchy.
  5. The skeleton is visible in the viewport (when "Show Skeleton"
     is on in the topbar).

Drop rig.ts alone first:

  1. Toast: 'Rig "rig.ts" queued — drop a model .ts next and it
     will attach automatically'
  2. Then drop model.ts:
  3. Toast: "Loading CubeCharacter.ts…"
  4. Toast: '✓ Queued rig "rig.ts" applied — 3 joints'

Drop model.ts + rig.ts + a 3D model (e.g. a GLB) in one batch:

  1. Model.ts loads → CURRENT_TS_MODEL set, rig attaches immediately.
  2. The GLB is APPENDED in a row to the right of the character
     (because the scene is non-empty when it drops). The camera
     refits to the combined bounding box.
  3. The rig only attaches to the TS model — the GLB is unaffected
     (PART 34 was the contract that introduces the rig pipeline,
     and it only applies to TS models with `userData.rigGraph`).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.5 — BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY: WHAT THE SINGLE-FILE PATH DOES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The single-file path is the SAME dispatch as before — `lblRouteFiles`
short-circuits to `lblRouteDroppedFile` which is the original
"image → texture, anything else → readFile" branch. So:

  - 1 model.ts   → unchanged (loads, sets CURRENT_TS_MODEL)
  - 1 rig.ts     → NEW: tries model first, fails, tries rig,
                   succeeds, queues (or applies if a model is
                   already in the scene)
  - 1 GLB/OBJ/etc → unchanged
  - 1 image      → unchanged (textures the selection)
  - 1 JSON/JS blueprint → unchanged
  - N of anything → NEW: bucketed + dispatched as documented above

The `multiple` attribute on the 4 file inputs is a no-op for the
single-file case (browsers just behave the same), so the change is
fully backwards-compatible at the file-picker level too.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.6 — PRE-EMISSION CHECKLIST (extends the 35.6 + 34.6 lists)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The 6-item pre-emission checklist in 35.6 and the 6-item PART 34
checklist are unchanged. If you write a .ts file that exposes a
RigGraph (PART 36), add these 2 items to your personal mental
checklist (not part of the official gate — the renderer doesn't
enforce them, they're here so you don't ship a broken rig file):

  [PART-36.1] If your .ts file has NO `create*Model` factory, the
              dropper will try to load it as a RIG. Make sure one of
              the 4 SHAPES in 36.2 is present, otherwise the user
              gets a "no RigGraph found" error.
  [PART-36.2] Every joint in the `joints` array needs a UNIQUE `id`,
              exactly ONE joint with `parentId: null` (the root), and
              every non-root joint's `parentId` must point at an id
              that appears EARLIER in the array (topological order).
              `three-rig-helpers.validateRigGraph()` enforces these.

PART 36 does NOT introduce a new export path, a new mandatory contract,
or a new validator on .ts factories. The new behavior is purely the
dropper's — a .ts file either IS a model (and goes through the existing
PART 1 pipeline) or IS a rig (and goes through 36.2). No file is
forced to be one or the other; the user picks by which file they
choose to drop.

PART 36 layers on top of, never replaces:
  - PART 1 (TS output contract) — every model still has a factory
  - PART 2 (type safety) — rig exports are untyped or self-typed
  - PART 4 (no relative imports) — same rule for rig files
  - PART 30 (rigging) — auto-promotion still happens for blueprints
  - PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers) — extended, not replaced
  - PART 34 (rig helpers) — rig files use the same `three-rig-helpers`
  - PART 35 (tris inspector) — multi-model scenes still report their
    combined triangle budget via the 🔺 Tris button


--------------------------------------------------------------------------
36.7 — FILE EXTENSION ROUTING TABLE + NAMING RULES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
This sub-subsection is the single source of truth for "what does
the dropper do with a file of extension X?" — useful when a user
asks "can I drop a .foo file?" or "does the file name matter?".

COMPLETE EXTENSION ROUTING TABLE (every extension the dropper
recognizes as of v1.13 / v8.5):

  Extension(s)        Bucket        Loader
  ──────────────────  ────────────  ──────────────────────────────────
  .glb, .gltf         3D model      GLTFLoader — load3DFile (append
                                   if scene non-empty)
  .fbx                3D model      FBXLoader — load3DFile
  .obj                3D model      OBJLoader — load3DFile
  .stl                3D model      STLLoader — load3DFile
  .dae                3D model      ColladaLoader — load3DFile
  .usdz               3D model      NOT supported in browser — toast
                                   "USDZ preview not supported in
                                   browser — try GLB/glTF instead"
  .png, .jpg, .jpeg,  image         lblApplyImageFileToSelected
  .gif, .webp, .bmp,                (textures the current selection
  .svg, .avif, .heic,               or spawns a textured plane)
  .heif
  .json               blueprint     validateAndBuild (JSON path)
                                   or model+rig fallback if the
                                   JSON happens to look like a
                                   RigGraph (rare — use .ts for
                                   rigs in practice)
  .js, .mjs           blueprint     validateAndBuild (JS path)
                                   or model+rig fallback
  .ts, .mts, .cts     blueprint     validateAndBuildTs → tries the
                                   create*Model factory first, falls
                                   back to the rig path on failure
  .zip                zip           __lblExtractZip → recurse on the
                                   extracted FileList
  (no extension or    blueprint     Last-resort fallback. Treated as
   unknown extension)               text. The TS pipeline will throw
                                   a parse error if it isn't valid
                                   JS/TS, but JSON and JS text are
                                   accepted too.

The bucket dispatch is purely extension-based. The basename is NOT
checked at this stage — `MyAwesomeCube.ts` and `model.ts` and
`foo.ts` all go to the same bucket. The bucket then calls the
loader, which is the one that decides what to do with the file's
CONTENT.

NAMING RULES — "does the file name matter?":

  Rule 1: For NON-TS files (3D models, images), the basename is
          COMPLETELY IGNORED. The file content is all that matters.
          `Hero.glb`, `low_poly_knight.glb`, `untitled.glb` — all
          load identically.

  Rule 2: For TS files, the basename is IGNORED too. The factory
          function NAME (not the file name) is what matters. The
          factory must match the `create*Model` pattern (e.g.
          `createCubeModel`, `createMinecraftCharacterModel`,
          `createMyAwesomeThingModel`) OR be the default export
          of a function that returns a `THREE.Object3D` with at
          least one child. See PART 1 for the full factory
          contract.

  Rule 3: For ZIP files, the basename is the bundle name (shown
          in the extraction toast). The contents are extracted and
          each entry is routed by ITS OWN extension (per Rule 1-2
          and the table above). A zip with `cube.ts` + `skeleton.ts`
          works exactly like a zip with `model.ts` + `rig.ts`.

  Rule 4: For the DEMO / MODEL STORE only (the "‹ Model N ›" buttons
          in the topbar), file names DO matter — the legacy probe
          and the manifest look for `Model_1`, `Model_2`, etc. in
          `public/models/`. This is the ONE place where the
          `Model_N.<ext>` naming convention is enforced. Direct
          user uploads (drop / file-picker / drag) are unaffected.

  Rule 5: There is NO required "primary" or "index" file inside a
          bundle. The first file the renderer successfully builds
          (the `create*Model` factory, in TS-extension order) becomes
          the model. Rig files attach to it, 3D models append to it,
          etc. The conventional `model.ts` name is just a
          readability convention, not a requirement.

EXAMPLES — every row loads identically:

  Direct drop 1: cube.ts + skeleton.ts
    → cube.ts has `create*Model` factory → loads as model
    → skeleton.ts has no factory, has RigGraph default → loads as rig
    → rig attaches to the cube model

  Direct drop 2: MyAwesomeModel.ts + my_rig.ts
    → identical behavior to drop 1 (basenames are ignored)

  Zip bundle 1: Bundle.zip / [model.ts + rig.ts + extra.glb]
    → extracted, then routed: model.ts → model, rig.ts → rig,
      extra.glb → 3D model appended in a row

  Zip bundle 2: Bundle.zip / [cube.ts + skeleton.ts + hero.glb]
    → identical behavior to bundle 1

  Direct drop 3: cube.ts + skeleton.ts + hero.glb
    → identical to dropping a zip containing the same three files

  Demo / Model Store: public/models/Model_1.zip
    → the "Model_1" in the file name is what the demo system
      looks for; once found, the .zip extension triggers
      extraction just like a direct drop

PART 36 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new top-level API surface (no `lblAttachRig` global — the
    queue + drain pattern is the only public hook)
  - A new pre-emission gate or validator item
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ loader
  - A change to the editor paste path (still single-file at a time)
  - A change to the Demo / Model Store (still one .ts per click)
  - A required naming convention for direct user uploads (the
    basename is always ignored; the factory function name and
    export shape are what matter — see 36.2 and the "naming rules"
    in 36.7)

PART 36 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new top-level API surface (no `lblAttachRig` global — the
    queue + drain pattern is the only public hook)
  - A new pre-emission gate or validator item
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ loader
  - A change to the editor paste path (still single-file at a time)
  - A change to the Demo / Model Store (still one .ts per click)

================================================================================
END OF PART 36 — v1.13 / v8.5 ADDITIONS: MULTI-FILE DROP + MODEL + RIG PAIRING
Multi-file drop is now first-class: the user can pick N files at once
or drop N files in one drag, the dropper buckets them by extension /
MIME, and 3D models get arranged in a row + camera-refit while
blueprints + rigs process sequentially. A new "rig" file type lets a
model.ts + rig.ts pair render together with zero changes to either
file (just write the rig as `export default { joints: [...] }` and
drop them in any order). Every existing single-file workflow is
preserved. The new behavior is purely additive on the dropper and
the renderer state — no new contract, no new validator, no new
mandatory field. Layers on top of PART 1, PART 2, PART 4, PART 30,
PART 32 / PART 65, PART 34, PART 35. Nothing in this PART removes or
weakens any rule from PARTs 1-35.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 37 — v1.14 / v8.6 ADDITIONS: ZIP BUNDLE SUPPORT
The renderer accepts a `.zip` file (single file, drop, drag, or
served from the demo / model-store URL) as a "bundle" of model +
rig + any other files, and extracts it in-memory before routing
each entry through the same multi-file drop pipeline from PART 36.
The user can ship one zip per model (e.g. `Model_1.zip`) instead
of juggling separate files. This PART layers on top of PART 1
(single-file factory output contract), PART 2 (types), PART 4
(imports), PART 30 (rigging), PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical
helpers + validators), PART 34 (pre-bundled rig helpers), PART 35
(triangle-first geometry), and PART 36 (multi-file drop + model +
rig pairing). The renderer gains:
  - `fflate` in the import map (10 KB minified, no deps, MIT)
  - `multiple` attribute on the 4 file inputs extended with `.zip`
  - a new "zip" file-type bucket in the dispatcher
  - `__lblExtractZip(file)` that turns a zip into a list of File
    objects ready for the existing routing pipeline
  - `__lblLooksLikeZip(bytes)` magic-byte check (`PK\x03\x04`)
  - `__lblFetchBytes(url)` for the demo / model-store path
  - `.zip` accepted in the demo manifest + the legacy probe
  - the model-store "Download Model Code" button re-serves the
    original zip bytes verbatim (no inner-model extraction)
================================================================================

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.1 — EXECUTIVE OVERVIEW: WHAT A BUNDLE LOOKS LIKE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A bundle is just a regular `.zip` archive. The renderer does not
impose a particular layout, but the conventional / recommended shape
matches the demo / model-store naming and the PART 36 model + rig
pairing contract:

  Model_1.zip                  (or any *.zip — the file's basename is
                                irrelevant, only the contents matter)
  ├── model.ts                 (REQUIRED — the main model factory.
                                Can also be named model.js / model.json
                                if you prefer the JSON/JS blueprint
                                path. Whichever file the parser
                                accepts as a model goes first.)
  ├── rig.ts                   (OPTIONAL — PART 36's 4 export shapes.
                                Auto-attaches to the just-loaded
                                model exactly as if you'd dropped
                                it standalone.)
  ├── extra.glb                (OPTIONAL — appended in a row the
                                same way a multi-file GLB drop would.)
  ├── texture.png              (OPTIONAL — applied as a texture to
                                the selection, same as a regular
                                image drop.)
  ├── README.md                (OPTIONAL — informational only; the
                                renderer doesn't try to render it
                                but lists it in the extraction toast
                                so the user knows it's there.)
  └── <any other asset>        (OPTIONAL — anything the import map
                                can route is processed; unknown
                                extensions are passed through as
                                blueprint text last-resort, same
                                as a regular drop.)

The renderer filters out macOS resource-fork junk (`__MACOSX/.*`),
hidden dotfiles (`.DS_Store`, `._*`), and directory markers. Every
other entry becomes a File object and goes through `lblRouteFiles`.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.2 — DROP, FILE-PICKER, AND DRAG-AND-DROP BEHAVIOR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A `.zip` is now a first-class file type in the dropper. All 4 file
inputs (`file-input`, `file3d-input`, `dz-input`, `mob-dz-input`)
include `.zip` in their `accept` attribute, and the dropzone
sub-text reads "drop a .zip with model.ts + rig.ts" so the user
knows the feature exists. Drop / pick / drag behavior:

  1. Single .zip dropped   → `__lblExtractZip` runs, the extracted
                              files re-enter `lblRouteFiles`, which
                              fans them out as if they'd been dropped
                              individually. The user sees:
                              "✓ Extracted N files from Model_1.zip"
  2. .zip + other files     → Zips are processed FIRST (so the
                              extraction completes before the other
                              files start), then the non-zip files
                              go through the existing bucket dispatch.
  3. Multiple .zips         → Extracted sequentially (the second zip
                              waits for the first to finish) so a
                              model.ts in zip A + a rig.ts in zip B
                              still wire up via `__lblPendingRigs`.

The full extraction → routing path is:

  ┌─ drop / pick zip
  │
  ├─ __lblClassifyFile → 'zip' bucket
  │
  ├─ async __lblExtractZip(file):
  │     unzip() via fflate
  │     filter out __MACOSX, .DS_Store, dotfiles
  │     text files → File(text); binary files → File(uint8)
  │     return { files, entries, skipped, zipName }
  │
  ├─ toast "Extracted N files from <zipName>"
  │
  └─ lblRouteFiles(extracted) — recurse with the FileList
        ├─ model.ts   → validateAndBuild →  CURRENT_TS_MODEL set
        ├─ rig.ts     → __lblReadBlueprintOrRig → rig attaches
        ├─ extra.glb  → load3DFile (append if scene non-empty)
        ├─ texture    → lblApplyImageFileToSelected
        └─ README.md  → classified 'blueprint' last-resort;
                        try-compile fails with a parse error that
                        shows up as a non-blocking toast (the
                        model + rig still load — README.md is the
                        user's documentation, not source code)

If `__lblExtractZip` itself throws (corrupt zip, password-protected,
unsupported compression method), the user sees a clear error toast
with the underlying message and the rest of the batch is unaffected.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.3 — DEMO / MODEL-STORE BEHAVIOR
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The demo system (Demo / Model Store) is also zip-aware. A demo
author can ship a single `public/models/Model_1.zip` and the
renderer will:

  1. Discover it via the manifest:
     { "id": "Model_1", "file": "Model_1.zip" }
     (the loader accepts .zip alongside .ts / .js / .json)

  2. Or discover it via the legacy probe (which now tries
     `Model_1.zip` after `Model_1.ts` / `.js` / `.json`).

  3. On click, fetch the zip, check the `PK\x03\x04` magic bytes
     (so a stale 404 HTML page isn't mistaken for a zip), then
     extract + route through the same multi-file dispatch.

  4. The "Download Model Code" button re-serves the **original
     zip bytes verbatim** — what you download is what you
     uploaded, not a reconstructed model.ts. This is important
     for a demo workflow: if the user finds a bug in a model
     bundle, they can fix the source files in the zip, re-upload
     to the repo, and the next demo load serves back exactly
     the same archive. The download goes through the existing
     `lblLastDemoSource.bytes` path, no new button needed.

The legacy probe prefers .ts over .zip when both exist for the
same Model_N, so a repo with `Model_1.ts` keeps using the source
editor flow and a repo with only `Model_1.zip` (no source) still
loads. To FORCE zip mode, list the .zip in the manifest.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.4 — WHAT A BUNDLE FILE MUST NOT DO
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The PART 4 single-file / no-relative-imports rules apply to every
.ts / .js / .json file inside a bundle, exactly as if they'd been
dropped individually:

  - No relative imports to OTHER FILES IN THE SAME BUNDLE. The
    extraction produces flat File objects, and the TS pipeline
    rewrites imports through the import map — `./rig` inside a
    bundle does NOT resolve to the bundle's rig.ts.
  - Imports of `'three'`, `'three/addons/...'`, `'three-bvh-csg'`,
    `'three-rig-helpers'`, `'fflate'` (and any future import-map
    entries) are allowed.

If you need to share helpers between model.ts and rig.ts inside
a bundle, the supported workaround is:

  1. Inline the helper functions into each file (the spec already
     encourages this — the LBL spec is a self-contained-output
     format, see PART 4.1 "no relative imports").
  2. OR factor the helper into a third file, write a tiny build
     step that concatenates everything into one model.ts at
     deploy time, and ship the .ts in the demo store instead
     of the .zip.

The renderer does NOT enforce this with a separate linter — the
existing PART 4 validators handle it on the model side, and the
dynamic-import failure surfaces naturally on the rig side.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.5 — BACKWARDS COMPATIBILITY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
A bundle is purely additive. Every existing workflow is preserved:

  - Single .ts / .js / .json / .glb / .obj / .stl / .fbx / .dae /
    image drop → unchanged.
  - Multi-file drop with model + rig → unchanged (PART 36 still
    works exactly as before, zips are just one more file type
    that the bucket dispatch recognizes).
  - Demo / model-store with .ts / .js / .json → unchanged.
  - Editor paste path → unchanged (still single-file at a time).
  - "Download Model Code" button → serves .ts / .js / .json as
    text (unchanged) AND zip as `application/zip` (new — only
    when the source was a zip).

The `accept` attribute change on the 4 file inputs (added `.zip`)
is a no-op for users who don't have zips — the browser's file
picker shows zip files alongside the other supported extensions
but the user can still pick a non-zip file and the existing flow
takes over.

The `__lblClassifyFile` change (added the 'zip' bucket) is also
a no-op for non-zip files. The bucket only triggers when the file
extension is `.zip`, and the extracted File objects are re-classified
in the recursive `lblRouteFiles` call with their original
extensions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.6 — PRE-EMISSION CHECKLIST (extends the 36.6 / 35.6 / 34.6 lists)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
The 6-item pre-emission checklist in 35.6, the 6-item PART 34
checklist, and the 2-item PART 36 voluntary checklist are all
unchanged. If you ship a bundle (PART 37), add these 2 voluntary
items to your personal mental checklist (not enforced by any
validator — the renderer doesn't introspect zip layouts):

  [PART-37.1] The bundle contains at least one .ts / .js / .json
              file that defines a renderable scene. An empty zip
              gets a "contained no renderable files" error.
  [PART-37.2] The bundle's .ts / .js / .json files do NOT use
              relative imports to other files in the same zip.
              The import map doesn't know about zip-internal
              paths. (See 37.4 for the supported workarounds.)

PART 37 does NOT introduce a new mandatory field, a new export
path, a new validator, or a new API surface. The new behavior is
purely a new input format on the dropper + a new recognized
extension in the demo / model-store. No .ts factory is required
to know about zips — the renderer handles extraction transparently.

PART 37 layers on top of, never replaces:
  - PART 1 (TS output contract) — every bundle's model still has a factory
  - PART 2 (type safety) — same TypeScript rules
  - PART 4 (no relative imports) — applies to zip contents
  - PART 30 (rigging) — auto-promotion still happens for blueprints
  - PART 32 / PART 65 (canonical helpers) — extended, not replaced
  - PART 34 (rig helpers) — bundles can include rig.ts that uses
    'three-rig-helpers' exactly like a standalone rig file
  - PART 35 (tris inspector) — bundle-loaded models still report
    their triangle budget via the 🔺 Tris button
  - PART 36 (multi-file drop) — the zip extraction feeds into the
    same `lblRouteFiles` dispatcher


--------------------------------------------------------------------------
37.7 — BUNDLE CONTENT NAMING RULES + PER-ENTRY ROUTING TABLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
This sub-subsection is the zip-specific companion to PART 36.7.
It documents "what does the renderer do with a file named
`foo.bar` inside a zip bundle?" — useful when a user asks "can I
name my model file anything inside the zip?" or "what if I have
two `.ts` files in the same bundle, which is the model?".

PER-ENTRY ROUTING TABLE (every extension a zip entry can have
and what the renderer does with it):

  Entry extension     What the renderer does
  ──────────────────  ──────────────────────────────────────────
  .ts / .mts / .cts   Run the regular model pipeline (try
                      create*Model factory). If that fails,
                      retry as a rig (see PART 36.2 for the 4
                      export shapes). The first entry to
                      successfully build a model becomes THE
                      model of the bundle; subsequent .ts
                      entries are either rigs (if they expose
                      a RigGraph) or extra blueprints (rare,
                      shows up as a parse error toast).

  .js / .mjs          Run the regular JS blueprint pipeline. No
                      rig fallback for plain .js (only TS gets
                      the rig fallback — see 36.2 for why).

  .json               Run the regular JSON blueprint pipeline.
                      No rig fallback (rare in practice; a rig
                      is almost always a .ts file).

  .glb / .gltf /      Append in a row to the right of the model
  .fbx / .obj /       (3D models use the same `load3DFile`
  .stl / .dae         append path as a direct multi-file drop).
                      If the bundle has no model.ts (only 3D
                      models + maybe a rig), the first 3D
                      model becomes the "anchor" at origin
                      and the rest append to it.

  .png / .jpg /       Apply as a texture to the current
  .jpeg / .gif /      selection. If nothing is selected, the
  .webp / .bmp /      renderer spawns a new textured plane
  .svg / .avif /      (same as a direct image drop).
  .heic / .heif

  .zip                NESTED ZIPS are not supported. The first
                      .zip inside a .zip is treated as blueprint
                      text and will fail to parse. Users who
                      need a multi-bundle workflow should drop
                      multiple .zip files in one drop instead
                      (the dispatcher processes them
                      sequentially per PART 37.2).

  .md / .txt /        Treated as blueprint text last-resort
  .html / .css /      (per PART 36.7). JSON-shaped .json5/.yaml
  .xml / .yaml /      / .toml files fall through the same
  .yml / .map         path. Unknown extensions also fall
                      through — the parser tries, fails with a
                      parse error, the rest of the bundle is
                      unaffected.

  __MACOSX/.*         FILTERED OUT (macOS resource-fork junk).
                      The user sees them in console.log only,
                      not in the extraction toast.
  .DS_Store,          FILTERED OUT (hidden dotfiles).
  ._* (dotfiles)

NAMING RULES — "what should I name the files inside my bundle?":

  Rule 1: ANY NAME WORKS for a direct user upload (drop / file-
          picker / drag). `cube.ts` + `skeleton.ts` is exactly
          the same as `model.ts` + `rig.ts`. The basenames are
          ignored — the renderer classifies by EXTENSION and
          builds by CONTENT.

  Rule 2: For DEMO / MODEL STORE files (public/models/Model_N.*),
          the name `Model_N.<ext>` IS required (the legacy probe
          and the manifest lookup use this convention). This
          rule applies regardless of whether the demo file is
          a single .ts, a single .json, a single .glb, or a
          full .zip bundle.

  Rule 3: There is no "primary" or "index" file requirement
          inside a bundle. The renderer doesn't look for a
          file named `model.ts` or `index.ts`. Instead:
          - The first .ts that successfully builds via the
            model pipeline becomes the model
          - Any other .ts that fails as a model is retried as
            a rig and attaches to the just-loaded model
          - If the bundle has no .ts at all but does have 3D
            models, the first 3D model becomes the anchor and
            the rest append to it

  Rule 4: The CONVENTIONAL layout (recommended for readability
          + tooling) is:

            Model_1.zip (or any name for direct drops)
            ├── model.ts          (the factory)
            ├── rig.ts            (optional — auto-attaches)
            ├── extra.glb         (optional — 3D model)
            ├── texture.png       (optional — texture)
            └── README.md         (optional — documentation)

          But the renderer will happily load a bundle with any
          other layout, as long as the contents have the right
          extensions and the right export shapes.

EXAMPLES — every row extracts + routes identically:

  Bundle 1: Bundle.zip / [model.ts + rig.ts + hero.glb + grass.png]
    → 4 files extracted, 0 junk
    → model.ts loads as the cube
    → rig.ts attaches to the cube
    → hero.glb appends in a row
    → grass.png textures the selection (or spawns a plane)

  Bundle 2: Bundle.zip / [cube.ts + skeleton.ts + extra.obj + note.md]
    → identical behavior (basenames don't matter)

  Bundle 3: Bundle.zip / [hero.glb + decoration.glb + notes.md]
    → no model.ts, so the first .glb (hero.glb) anchors at origin
    → decoration.glb appends in a row
    → notes.md fails to parse as blueprint, toast warns

  Bundle 4: Bundle.zip / [model.ts + model2.ts + rig.ts]
    → model.ts loads first (factory succeeds)
    → model2.ts — if it has a factory, it REPLACES the scene
      (current behavior — last-load-wins); if it has no
      factory, it's tried as a rig and attaches to model.ts
    → rig.ts attaches to whichever model is currently in the
      scene after model2.ts is processed

  Bundle 5: Bundle.zip / [rig.ts]  (model dropped second, separately)
    → rig.ts has no model to attach to → queued in
      __lblPendingRigs
    → User drops model.ts (or another zip with model.ts) →
      rig drains and attaches
    → See PART 36.2 for the queue/drain protocol

PART 37 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new top-level API surface (no global `lblLoadZipFromUrl` —
    the demo flow uses the existing `lblModelStoreLoadByNumber`)
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE loader
  - A change to the editor paste path
  - A change to the file-picker UI (just an `accept` attr extension)
  - A change to the scene tree or the Rig button
  - A dependency on a non-import-mapped module (fflate IS in
    the import map, same access pattern as the other deps)
  - A required "primary" file name inside a bundle (Rule 3
    above — there's no `model.ts` requirement)

PART 37 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new top-level API surface (no global `lblLoadZipFromUrl` —
    the demo flow uses the existing `lblModelStoreLoadByNumber`)
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE loader
  - A change to the editor paste path
  - A change to the file-picker UI (just an `accept` attr extension)
  - A change to the scene tree or the Rig button
  - A dependency on a non-import-mapped module (fflate IS in
    the import map, same access pattern as the other deps)

================================================================================
END OF PART 37 — v1.14 / v8.6 ADDITIONS: ZIP BUNDLE SUPPORT
The renderer now accepts a .zip anywhere a file can enter the
system: drop, file-picker, drag, or the demo / model-store URL.
The bundle is extracted in-memory (no disk write), filtered for
junk files, and every entry is routed through the same multi-
file dispatcher from PART 36 — model.ts loads, rig.ts attaches,
extra GLBs append, images texture, READMEs surface as non-blocking
toast. The raw zip bytes are preserved so "Download Model Code"
re-serves the exact same archive the user loaded. Single-file
workflows are unchanged. Layers on top of PART 1, PART 2, PART
4, PART 30, PART 32 / PART 65, PART 34, PART 35, PART 36. Nothing
in this PART removes or weakens any rule from PARTs 1-36.
================================================================================
================================================================================
PART 38 — v1.15 / v8.7 ADDITIONS: CHARACTER PIPELINE UPGRADE
This PART is an UPGRADE GUIDE. It does NOT replace any rule from
PARTs 1-37 (TS) / 1-66 (JSON/JS). It is the answer to a recurring
diagnosis the maintainer reported against the renderer's existing
character outputs: more polygons do not automatically create a better
model. The character looked assembled, not modeled. Joints overlapped
as primitives, hands were mitten-like, faces were flat, clothing
floated, materials were uniform, and a single camera view hid most
of the problems. PART 38 teaches the AI how to upgrade its character
output from primitive-assembly to a professional character pipeline:
continuous topology, deliberate edge loops, fitted clothing, real
skeletal skinning, named animation clips, GLB export, and multi-view
validation. The PART is the spec-side counterpart to the same
upgrade guide the maintainer pasted as input. The guide's central
principle is the first thing this PART records and the last thing
it reminds the AI of before emission.

Source: 3D CHARACTER GENERATION SYSTEM UPGRADE GUIDE (the txt file
the maintainer attached when requesting this upgrade). Bumped from
v1.14 / v8.6 to v1.15 / v8.7.
================================================================================

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.0 — THE CENTRAL PRINCIPLE (READ THIS BEFORE EVERY CHARACTER EMISSION)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

    More polygons do not automatically create a better model.

A model made from many high-segment spheres, cones, and cylinders
is still a collection of primitives. Professional quality comes from
continuous topology, deliberate silhouettes, deformation-aware edge
loops, consistent proportions, UVs, texture variation, real skeletal
skinning, animation testing, and multi-view validation — NOT from
raw vertex count.

If you are about to emit a character and your plan is "add more
segments to the existing primitives", STOP and re-plan. Increase
polygon count deliberately, on the parts of the silhouette that
actually need it (ears, hands, eyes, shoulders, elbows, hips, knees,
armor edges, cloth hems), and decrease it where it does not help.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.1 — DIAGNOSIS OF THE PRIMITIVE-ASSEMBLY APPROACH
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

The classic approach — place independent primitives into a hierarchy
of THREE.Group pivots — is fine for low-poly props and quick blockouts,
but it is NOT a professional character-authoring method. PART 30's
pivot hierarchy is a valid runtime choice for jointed poses, but the
character is being MODELLED as if every body part were an unrelated
ellipsoid. The ten visible symptoms are:

  1. The head, torso, arms, and legs look assembled rather than modeled.
  2. Joints appear as overlapping spheres or cylinders instead of
     connected anatomy.
  3. Increasing segment counts increases geometry density but does
     not improve the actual form.
  4. The face lacks continuous eyelid, cheek, mouth, and jaw topology.
  5. Hands and feet remain mitten-like because they do not have proper
     finger and toe structure.
  6. Armor and clothing are separate visual blocks instead of layered
     garments fitted to a body.
  7. Transform pivots provide basic motion but do not deform a
     continuous skin mesh.
  8. Flat material colors make the result appear like a technical
     prototype.
  9. A single camera view hides problems in the side, rear, and
     animated poses.
  10. The factory becomes too large and difficult to maintain when
     used as a full sculpting environment.

The fix is NOT to abandon the existing factory pattern. The fix is
to divide work into TWO layers (PART 38.3) and to follow the 14-stage
production pipeline (PART 38.4) instead of emitting a one-shot primitive
assembly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.2 — WHEN THIS PART APPLIES (AND WHEN IT DOES NOT)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

PART 38 only binds when the subject is a CHARACTER, MASCOT, CREATURE,
or HUMANOID — not a prop, vehicle, food, building, or abstract shape.
For non-character subjects, PART 38 is informational and the existing
PARTs 1-37 still govern.

A subject is "character-class" if any of the following is true:

  - The user prompt asks for a person, humanoid, creature, monster,
    goblin, orc, knight, mage, robot with a face, animal with
    recognizable facial identity, or similar.
  - The subject has a face (eye sockets, mouth, jaw topology).
  - The subject has identifiable limbs (arms with elbows, legs with
    knees, articulated hands).
  - The subject needs to deform believably under animation.

For a clearly mechanical subject (vehicle, turret, drone, sentry
without a face), the existing PART 30 pivot hierarchy + PART 33
features remain the default and PART 38.5-38.14 are skipped. PART 38.0,
38.1, 38.15, 38.16, 38.20, and 38.21 still apply as design discipline.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.3 — TWO-LAYER ARCHITECTURE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Divide the system into two layers, even inside a single .ts file:

  Asset-authoring layer (this PART's primary focus):
    - Creates the actual mesh, UVs, textures, skeleton, weights, and
      named animation clips.
    - Lives in the factory's body (PART 1.2 / PART 5.x).
    - Owns topology, materials, the rig, and the animation library.

  Viewer-integration layer (PART 1 + PART 30 + PART 5 + PART 6):
    - Loads the asset, exposes the runtime contract (sculptRuntime),
      creates named sockets, controls animations, supplies look-dev
      lights, and runs validation.
    - Lives in the factory's tail (after the geometry is built) plus
      the inspector / renderer code.

The TypeScript file's recommended internal layout (PART 1.4) already
supports this split: build geometry → build materials → build rig →
build clips → expose runtime. PART 38 reinforces that order. A factory
that builds the rig BEFORE the geometry it deforms is a build-order
bug and will not export cleanly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.4 — 14-STAGE PRODUCTION PIPELINE + 7 QUALITY GATES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

For every character subject, walk the following stages in order. Do
not skip stages. Each stage should produce a reviewable intermediate
result, not just a mental checkpoint.

  1.  Character specification     (PART 38.5)
  2.  Design and proportion blockout
  3.  Continuous base mesh        (PART 38.6)
  4.  Sculpt and secondary forms
  5.  Retopology and edge-loop cleanup (PART 38.7)
  6.  UV unwrap and texture atlas (PART 38.11)
  7.  Material authoring
  8.  Skeleton creation           (PART 38.12)
  9.  Skin weights and deformation cleanup
  10. Animation authoring         (PART 38.13)
  11. GLB/GLTF export             (PART 38.14)
  12. TypeScript viewer adapter
  13. Multi-view validation       (PART 38.17)
  14. Optimization and LOD generation (PART 38.18)

Quality gates (each is a CHECK that must pass before the next stage
opens; do not silently skip):

  Blockout gate    — silhouette reads from front, side, back, 3/4
  Form gate        — body, face, clothing, armor, props have volume
                     and clean attachments
  Topology gate    — edge loops exist at joints + face features;
                     no degenerate faces; quads dominate deforming
                     areas
  Material gate    — skin / cloth / leather / metal / bone / magic
                     have different roughness + color behavior
  Rig gate         — deforming vertices have valid normalized weights;
                     skeleton has a predictable hierarchy (PART 38.12)
  Animation gate   — idle / walk / attack / hit clips do not collapse
                     shoulders, elbows, hips, or knees (PART 38.13)
  Runtime gate     — asset loads, exposes named runtime nodes and
                     sockets, animates inside the viewer (PART 38.14)

The "single-pass emit and inspect at the end" workflow that the
diagnosis called out (PART 38.1 item 10) is what the gates exist to
prevent.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.5 — CHARACTER SPECIFICATION SCHEMA
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Before generating geometry, create a structured character spec. It
is a plain TypeScript const or JSON literal — separate from the mesh-
building code. PART 38 RECOMMENDS the following fields; the existing
PART 5.6 (sculptRuntime) contract continues to govern what the
runtime sees. The character spec is INTERNAL — it never needs to be
exposed on userData; it is the planning document the AI uses before
it writes geometry.

  identity: {
    name, species, ageCategory, personality, faction, role,
    visualKeywords  // e.g. "scarred goblin scout with bone charms"
  }

  proportions: {
    totalHeight, headHeight, shoulderWidth, chestDepth, hipWidth,
    upperArmLength, forearmLength, thighLength, shinLength,
    footLength
  }

  silhouette: {
    headShape, earLength, earAngle, shoulderShape, torsoTaper,
    limbThickness, footShape, posture, asymmetry
  }

  face: {
    eyeSize, eyeSpacing, browAngle, noseLength, jawWidth,
    mouthWidth, tuskSize, cheekShape, facialMarkings
  }

  costume: {
    garmentLayers, armorPieces, straps, belt, backpack, jewelry,
    materialAssignments, damageLevel, dirtLevel
  }

  palette: {
    skin, cloth, leather, metal, accent, emissive
  }

  rig: {
    requiredBones, optionalBones, sockets, constraints,
    deformationZones
  }

  animation: {
    idle, walk, attack, hit, death, expression, interaction
  }

  budget: {
    targetVertices, targetTriangles, maxMaterials, maxTextureSize,
    maxDrawCalls, lodTargets
  }

The spec is the answer to "what character am I making?" If you cannot
fill the schema, the prompt is too vague — ask the user one focused
question, then proceed. The schema is also the lens the multi-view
review uses (PART 38.17) to flag deviations: it knows what the eyes,
ears, shoulders, and hands were SUPPOSED to be, so it can compare.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.6 — CONTINUOUS MESH GENERATION (4 APPROACHES)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

The main anatomy should be CONNECTED meshes rather than unrelated
ellipsoids. Pick one of the four approaches below (or a documented
hybrid) before writing the geometry. The default for this renderer
is Approach D (hybrid), because the system ships a static TS factory
and a runtime that loads GLB.

  Approach A — Parametric body topology:
    Create a reusable humanoid topology template with rings and
    loops for torso, neck, shoulders, arms, hips, legs, hands, feet.
    Move the vertices based on the character spec.
    Best for: humanoid characters with consistent anatomy.

  Approach B — Lofted cross-sections:
    Define cross-sections along the body and connect them into a
    surface. Torso = elliptical rings at pelvis, waist, chest,
    shoulders. Arm = rings from shoulder to wrist. Rings MUST have
    consistent vertex counts so they connect as quads.
    Best for: stylized characters with non-standard proportions.

  Approach C — Implicit surface / voxel blockout + retopology:
    For highly variable body shapes, generate an implicit surface
    or voxel volume, extract an initial mesh, smooth it, run a
    retopology step. More complex; allows unusual creatures and
    exaggerated fantasy anatomy.
    Best for: creatures, exaggerated fantasy anatomy, monstrosities.

  Approach D — Hybrid (RECOMMENDED for this renderer):
    Use a professionally authored base topology for the body and
    head (the existing PART 30 pivot groups are the start point).
    Generate proportions and colors procedurally from the spec
    (PART 38.5). Create clothing and props procedurally as fitted
    layers (PART 38.10). Export a GLB and load it through TypeScript
    if the geometry exceeds ~10k triangles, otherwise keep it inline.
    This is the most common path in this renderer.

Whichever approach you choose, the OUTPUT the inspector sees is the
same: a single THREE.Group root with named child Groups for each
anatomical region, real geometry (not lollipops), and named sockets.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.7 — TOPOLOGY TECHNIQUES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Use polygons where they improve the silhouette or deformation. Do
NOT distribute them uniformly. Edge loops go in these locations:

  Face:
    - eye socket loops (around the eye inset, not on the surface)
    - eyelid loops (one for the upper lid, one for the lower lid)
    - mouth loops (one at the lip line, one below the chin)
    - cheek loops (subtle, two per side)
    - jaw loop (along the underside of the jaw)
    - nose support loops (around the nostrils, not through them)
    - ear attachment loops (where the ear meets the head)

  Shoulders:
    - clavicle loop (across the collarbone)
    - armpit loop (one extra ring of density for the underarm)
    - deltoid loop (around the deltoid muscle cap)
    - upper-arm twist loop (one extra ring of density to absorb
      twisting without pinching)

  Elbows:
    - at least two support loops around the elbow bend (one above,
      one below). Without these, the elbow pinches on bend.

  Hips:
    - pelvis loop (around the hip bones)
    - gluteal loop (under the gluteal fold)
    - hip socket loop (around the ball joint)
    - upper-leg twist loop (one extra ring for twisting)

  Knees:
    - kneecap loop (around the patella)
    - rear-knee compression loops (two extra rings behind the knee
      to absorb bend compression)

  Wrists and ankles:
    - support loops for rotation and taper (one per side)

Quad-dominant body. Triangles are acceptable in rigid armor, small
props, and areas that do not deform. Avoid long thin triangles and
sliver faces. Canonical cleanup operations (run before export):

  - weld duplicate vertices (use BufferGeometryUtils.mergeVertices)
  - remove degenerate triangles
  - recalculate normals (computeVertexNormals after weld)
  - unify winding order (assert all faces are CCW)
  - validate UV ranges (every UV in [0, 1])
  - validate material indices (no out-of-range indices)
  - validate bounding boxes (no NaN, no infinite values)
  - validate disconnected components (no orphan vertices)
  - detect non-manifold edges (no edge shared by > 2 faces)
  - detect zero-area faces (no faces with cross product ~ 0)

These checks are also the SHAPE of the geometry validators in
PART 38.16; emit a non-blocking warning when any of them fails on
the produced model.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.8 — POLYGON & VERTEX BUDGETING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

For a 10,000-20,000 vertex fantasy character, use a deliberate
allocation. Do NOT just increase every primitive segment count.

  Recommended allocation (parts of the total):

    Head and face                20-28 %
    Torso and connected limbs    32-40 %
    Hands and feet                8-12 %
    Clothing and armor           15-22 %
    Weapons and props             6-10 %
    Small secondary detail        3- 6 %

  Practical target for this renderer (single character, inline TS):

    Vertices    ~ 12,000 - 18,000
    Triangles   ~ 18,000 - 28,000
    Materials   <= 8 (atlas when possible)
    Draw calls  <= 12

  Hard cap reminder: PART 35 sets a 50,000-triangle soft cap. If you
  exceed it, mergeGeometries + decimate before claiming the model is
  ready (PART 35.2). A character that exceeds the soft cap is a
  signal that the budget was not allocated, not that the character
  needed the triangles.

  Do NOT spend the budget on hidden geometry. Prioritize:
    - silhouette-critical ears, horns, noses, shoulders, hands, feet
    - facial planes and eyes
    - shoulder, elbow, hip, knee deformation areas
    - armor edges and cloth hems
    - visible asymmetry and identity-defining features

  When in doubt, spend vertices on the SILHOUETTE, not the SURFACE.
  A character with a clean silhouette and a plain material reads as
  better than a character with a noisy surface and a confused silhouette.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.9 — FACIAL MODELING SYSTEM
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

The face is the strongest quality multiplier. A simple body with a
good face looks convincing; a detailed body with a primitive face
looks unfinished. Build the face using these 10 steps, in order:

  1. Build a continuous head mesh (NOT a sphere with a face glued on).
  2. Add INSET eye sockets (the eye sits inside the head, not on
     the surface).
  3. Create eyelid loops around the eyes (upper + lower).
  4. Shape cheek planes and the brow ridge (deliberate planar
     transitions, not a single rounded mass).
  5. Add a jaw and chin transition (a real edge, not a smooth blend).
  6. Build the nose from connected planes or a controlled insert
     (not a single cone).
  7. Add a mouth cavity, lip planes, and tooth or tusk sockets.
  8. Use asymmetry intentionally but subtly (a 1-2 mm offset
     between left and right reads as "real" without reading as
     "broken").
  9. Use separate iris and pupil geometry, or a face texture.
     One color = dead eyes.
  10. Add facial blend shapes for blink, brow, jaw, snarl, and
      pain (see PART 38.13 for animation wiring).

Goblin identity features (when the subject is a goblin) — use these
deliberately, not as a checklist of how many you can fit:

  - large but not oversized eyes (oversized = cartoon, not goblin)
  - angled brow ridge (sharp inward angle)
  - broad or hooked nose
  - short muzzle area (a long muzzle reads as a different species)
  - cheek planes (slight inward angle, two planes per side)
  - pointed ears angled outward
  - controlled tusks (two lower tusks, sized to the head; do not
    let them dominate the silhouette)
  - uneven teeth or facial scars
  - slightly asymmetrical jaw
  - subtle color variation around the nose, ears, cheeks, and eyes
    (achieved via vertex color or face texture, not flat material)

Avoid the temptation to over-scale ears or tusks. A 1.5x oversized
feature reads as costume, not character.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.10 — CLOTHING, ARMOR, AND PROPS
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Clothing is generated as FITTED LAYERS over the body, not floating
primitives. The technique in priority order:

  1. Offset and shrinkwrap a body region to create cloth layers
     (start from the body geometry, push vertices outward by the
     cloth thickness, then trim the openings at the seams).
  2. Add thickness with a controlled shell or solidify operation
     (every garment has an inner and outer face — single-sided
     geometry z-fights on the seams).
  3. Use edge loops around hems and seams (the hem and seam
     locations are the silhouette-critical detail; do not skip
     them).
  4. Create folds through directional displacement or sculpted
     vertex offsets (two or three major fold directions per
     garment, not noise).
  5. Keep straps and belts physically attached to their parent
     garment (a belt that floats above the waist is a visual bug).
  6. Create armor plates with bevels and consistent thickness
     (every plate is a chamfered box, not a flat quad).
  7. Use rivets and seams sparingly at visible hero locations
     (every rivet is a small cost in geometry; 30 rivets on a
     pauldron is too many).

Material hierarchy for a professional fantasy character (skin,
cloth, leather, metal, bone, magic) — each has a distinct
roughness + color profile:

  skin     : matte, slightly varied roughness (~ 0.6 - 0.8)
  cloth    : high roughness (~ 0.9), soft color variation
  leather  : medium-high roughness (~ 0.7 - 0.85), edge wear
  iron     : low roughness (~ 0.3 - 0.5), dark metallic response
  bronze   : warmer metallic response, similar roughness to iron
  bone     : warm off-white, high roughness (~ 0.8 - 0.9)
  magic    : emissive color with controlled bloom intensity

If you find yourself assigning the same material to skin and cloth,
stop — the inspector's E8 (material instance mutation) check plus
this rule say you have lost a quality dimension.

Props must be attached to SOCKETS or HAND BONES. Never leave a
weapon in world space while the character animates. The renderer's
socket naming convention (PART 33.1[7]) is:

  Socket_HeadTop       — hats, helms, hair, headpieces
  Socket_Arm_Left      — held weapons (left hand)
  Socket_Arm_Right     — held weapons (right hand)
  Socket_Backpack      — back-mounted items, capes, quivers

If your character holds a prop in a hand, parent the prop to the
appropriate hand bone / socket, NOT to the scene root. Props
parented to the scene root will NOT move with the character.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.11 — UVS AND TEXTURES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Add UV support as soon as the topology is stable. A texture atlas
is usually more efficient than a large number of independent
materials. The minimum professional texture set is:

  - albedo (or base color)     — REQUIRED
  - roughness                  — REQUIRED
  - normal (or bump)           — REQUIRED
  - metallic                   — OPTIONAL
  - emissive                   — OPTIONAL

A hand-painted fantasy style does not require photorealism. It
requires CONTROLLED VARIATION. Add:

  - darker creases (along seam and fold lines)
  - lighter planar highlights (on cheekbones, brow, shoulder caps)
  - dirt near the feet (gradient from ankle to knee, not uniform)
  - wear on armor edges (chamfered edge color brighter than flat
    face)
  - redness around ears and nose (small region, not the whole face)
  - color variation across the skin (per-vertex color or texture
    gradient, not flat)

Texture resolution: 1024 or 2048 atlas is generally more useful than
many small textures. Pack UV islands carefully and reserve space
for face features and hero props. The renderer's existing canvas-
texture helpers (PART 8.2 / PART 32) are the right tool for
procedural texture synthesis at runtime.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.12 — REAL SKELETAL RIGGING
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

For any character subject, REPLACE transform-only group animation
with a true skeletal rig for the deforming body. The renderer's
existing PART 30 pivot hierarchy is acceptable for static jointed
poses (PART 38.2 exception), but a character with continuous skin
should use a real SkinnedMesh.

  Recommended skeleton (REQUIRED bones):

    root
      pelvis
        spine
          chest
            neck
              head
                jaw
            clavicle_left
              upperArm_left
                forearm_left
                  hand_left
            clavicle_right
              upperArm_right
                forearm_right
                  hand_right
        thigh_left
          shin_left
            foot_left
        thigh_right
          shin_right
            foot_right

  Optional bones (add when the character needs them):

    fingers, toes, ears, tusks, backpack straps, weapon parts,
    facial controls (jaw + lip + brow + eyelid)

The body mesh must include:

  - skinIndex attribute (THREE.BoneIndex buffer attribute)
  - skinWeight attribute (THREE.BoneWeight buffer attribute)
  - normalized weights (sum to 1.0 per vertex within tolerance)
  - no vertices with zero total weight
  - no extreme weight spikes near joints (cap individual influence
    at ~ 0.8, distribute the remainder)

Use up to four influences per vertex. Clean weights manually or with
a weight-smoothing pass around shoulders, elbows, hips, knees,
wrists, and ankles.

For advanced quality, add corrective shape keys or corrective
rotation logic for:

  - shoulder lifting (avoids the deltoid collapse on arm raise)
  - elbow bending (avoids the elbow pinch on full flexion)
  - hip bending (avoids the gluteal pinch on leg raise)
  - knee bending (avoids the knee cap popping on full flexion)
  - neck turning (avoids the neck twist bulge)
  - jaw opening (avoids the chin collapsing)
  - goblin snarl (character-specific facial corrective)

The renderer's PART 34 pre-bundled `three-rig-helpers` library is
the SANCTIONED tool for skeleton creation in this codebase. The
existing E11 check enforces that any model declaring
`userData.rigGraph` also declares `userData.skeletonSource ===
"three-rig-helpers"` — that rule is preserved and re-affirmed here.
Models that use PART 30's pivot-hierarchy approach (no real
SkinnedMesh) do NOT need rigGraph and do NOT need skeletonSource.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.13 — ANIMATION TECHNIQUES
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Create animation clips from the skeleton, not only from rotating
parent groups. The minimum animation set:

  idle:
    - breathing (subtle chest scale modulation)
    - weight shift (subtle pelvis left-right)
    - eye blinking (eyelid blend shape)
    - subtle head motion (small noise on neck + head)

  walk:
    - alternating feet (one contact frame per step)
    - opposite arm swing (left arm forward when right leg forward)
    - pelvis motion (small lateral and rotational sway)
    - controlled foot contact (no sliding — see below)

  attack:
    - anticipation (wind-back pose, 0.2-0.3 s)
    - weapon wind-up (the strike preparation)
    - fast strike (the actual hit, 0.1-0.2 s)
    - follow-through (the recovery pose, 0.3-0.5 s)
    - recovery (back to idle)

  hit:
    - impact recoil (head + spine compression)
    - head reaction (slight backward + sideways)
    - spine compression (one frame of -5 % spine height)
    - arm response (involuntary limb motion)

Additional useful clips (add when the character has the need):

  run, crouch, jump, death, inspect_weapon, roar, snarl, pickup,
  hurt_left, hurt_right

Animation quality checks (the same shape as the validator's
Animation Gate from PART 38.4):

  - feet should NOT slide during walk
  - hands should remain attached to held props (parent to hand bone)
  - shoulders should NOT collapse during arm lifts
  - elbows should bend in a believable direction (knee-direction
    mirror)
  - knees should NOT invert (kneecap faces forward always)
  - head should remain attached during extreme poses
  - attack should have readable anticipation and follow-through
  - idle should remain subtle (not look like random vibration)

Store clips in the GLB when possible (see PART 38.14), then expose
them through the existing `sculptRuntime.animations` contract from
PART 5.6. The renderer's existing AnimationMixer wiring
(`root.userData.tick` + `sculptRuntime.animations.mixer`) is the
path of least resistance; the new rules in this PART do not change
that wiring.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.14 — GLB-FIRST ARCHITECTURE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Use GLB/GLTF as the primary interchange format for professional
character assets when the asset is large (over ~ 10k triangles),
authored outside the runtime, or shared between tools. GLB can
preserve the mesh, materials, UVs, skeleton, skin weights, animation
clips, and morph targets in a single file.

The TypeScript factory's role is then the VIEWER ADAPTER, not the
full asset author. The adapter's responsibilities:

  1. Load the GLB (use GLTFLoader from three/addons).
  2. Find named nodes (head, hand_left, hand_right, foot_left,
     foot_right, etc.).
  3. Find the skeleton and animation clips.
  4. Create runtime sockets (Socket_HeadTop, Socket_Arm_Left,
     Socket_Arm_Right, Socket_Backpack).
  5. Expose sculptRuntime metadata (PART 5.6) with the loaded
     asset's nodes / materials / animations.
  6. Expose animation names via the existing AnimationMixer
     contract.
  7. Apply model options (scale, shadows, wireframe, animation).
  8. Provide look-dev lights via the create*ModelLookDevLights
     factory (PART 1.3).
  9. Provide root.userData.tick for mixer updates.

The adapter does NOT manually construct the full body from
primitives when a GLB source exists. Keep primitive helpers for
small details, props, debug markers, and the fallback path (when
GLB loading fails, the factory can fall back to a low-poly
primitive assembly so the inspector still has something to show).

GLB export is the renderer's PART 30.5 + PART 32 responsibility
(the existing `exportRiggedModelToGlb` helper). The new content
in this PART is: when you build a character with > ~ 10k triangles
or with a real SkinnedMesh, plan to export to GLB and load it back,
rather than trying to inline the whole thing in the factory's source.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.15 — TOOLING OPTIONS (3 PATHS)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

  Path A — Blender-assisted pipeline (best for highest quality and
           lowest topology risk):
    Use Blender for modeling, retopology, UVs, materials, rigging,
    animation, and GLB export. Use the TypeScript system only for
    loading, inspection, runtime metadata, and presentation.

  Path B — Procedural mesh pipeline (best for fully generated
           characters with strong variation):
    Implement continuous topology templates, lofting, welding, UV
    generation, skeleton creation, automatic weights, and GLB
    export. Significantly more engineering; provides strong
    variation across many characters.

  Path C — Hybrid pipeline (RECOMMENDED for this renderer):
    Use reusable high-quality body and head templates, generate
    proportions and colors procedurally, create clothing and props
    procedurally as fitted layers, export to GLB if the asset is
    large, then load it through TypeScript. This is the most common
    path in this renderer and is the default that the existing
    PART 1 + PART 5 + PART 6 + PART 30 + PART 33 + PART 34 + PART
    35 + PART 36 + PART 37 + this PART 38 support.

Supporting libraries and tools (this renderer's current set):

  Three.js                      runtime rendering, GLTF loading,
                                animation mixers, skeletons, materials
  three / addons                GLTFLoader, GLTFExporter, helpers
  three-rig-helpers (PART 34)   skeleton + skin weights
  fflate (PART 37)              zip bundle extraction
  mergeGeometries               topology composition (PART 35)
  mergeVertices                 duplicate-vertex cleanup (PART 38.7)
  BufferGeometryUtils           vertex welding, normals, simplification
  Image / canvas textures       albedo, roughness, decals, masks
  (future) automated render     multi-view previews for validation

PART 38 does not introduce a new dependency. It tells the AI how
to USE the existing dependencies more deliberately on character
subjects.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.16 — VALIDATION SYSTEM
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

The geometry / rig / animation / material / presentation checks
the maintainer called for are listed below. Some are already
implemented in the renderer's E1-E12 validators; PART 38 ADDS the
character-specific checks (E13-E16) that the existing set did not
cover. All character-specific checks are non-blocking warnings,
not errors — the model still loads.

  Geometry checks:
    E1   relative imports (existing)            — already implemented
    E7   deprecated THREE.Geometry (existing)   — already implemented
    E12  triangle density / un-merged parts
         (existing, PART 35)                    — already implemented
    E13  vertex count inside the declared budget
         (character-specific)                   — NEW in this PART
    E14  no NaN / infinite vertex positions
         (character-specific)                   — NEW in this PART
    E15  no duplicate vertices beyond tolerance,
         no non-manifold edges, no zero-area faces
         (character-specific)                   — NEW in this PART
    E16  bounding box within expected proportions
         (character-specific)                   — NEW in this PART

  Rig checks:
    E11  rig helpers marker (existing)          — already implemented
    (character-specific, future) — bone names unique, hierarchy has
         one root, skin indices refer to valid bones, weights sum
         to ~ 1.0, no vertex has zero total influence, all sockets
         are descendants of the model root. The current E11 is a
         marker test; deeper rig checks ship in a future release.

  Animation checks:
    E2   animation track binding (existing)     — already implemented
    E3   normalized quaternions (existing)      — already implemented
    (character-specific, future) — all required clips exist, clips
         have nonzero duration, no clip contains invalid tracks,
         feet contact ground during walk, held props remain attached.

  Material checks:
    E5   canvas texture lifecycle (existing)    — already implemented
    E6   GLTF userData hygiene (existing)       — already implemented
    E8   material instance mutation (existing)  — already implemented
    (character-specific, future) — every mesh has a material, material
         count is controlled, texture references resolve, roughness
         and metalness values are sensible, emissive materials are
         limited to intended regions.

  Presentation checks (character-specific, future):
    - front, side, back, three-quarter renders
    - neutral, grazing, reference lighting modes
    - wireframe preview (already supported)
    - skeleton overlay (already supported via PART 34)
    - socket overlay (already supported via PART 33.1[7])
    - animation preview (already supported via the mixer)
    - mobile viewport preview

The four new validators (E13-E16) are added to ANTI_PATTERN_CHECKS
in index.html alongside the existing twelve. They are non-blocking;
their findings surface on the spec chip just like E1-E12 do today.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.17 — MULTI-VIEW AND SILHOUETTE REVIEW
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Do not evaluate the character from the default camera alone. A
character that reads well from the front but breaks on the side is
a broken character. The renderer's existing camera + look-dev
lighting surface PART 1.3 + PART 33 supports this when the author
uses them, but PART 38 makes the multi-view review a REQUIREMENT
for character subjects.

The required review angles for every character emission:

  - front
  - left side
  - right side
  - back
  - three-quarter front
  - three-quarter back
  - low camera
  - high camera
  - idle pose
  - walk contact pose
  - attack anticipation
  - attack impact
  - hit pose

Compare the character against the character spec (PART 38.5) using
landmarks:

  - eye centers
  - nose tip
  - chin
  - ear tips
  - shoulder corners
  - hand positions
  - knee centers
  - foot tips
  - weapon tip

When a landmark deviates from the spec by more than the budget
declared in PART 38.8, flag it as a non-blocking warning in the
spec chip. PART 38 does not implement automatic landmark detection
in this release; it instructs the AI to perform the review before
emission, and the renderer surfaces the existing spec chip as the
collection point for any human review notes.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.18 — LOD AND PERFORMANCE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

Create at least three levels of detail when the character is used
in a scene with multiple instances or wide camera movement:

  LOD0  10,000 - 20,000 vertices   hero view, close inspection
  LOD1   5,000 -  8,000 vertices   normal gameplay distance
  LOD2   1,500 -  3,000 vertices   distant views
