Keep the skeleton and animation behavior consistent across LODs.
Do not strip the rig from LOD1/LOD2 — a rigged LOD0 + un-rigged
LOD1 produces a visible pop on LOD switch.

Use texture compression and reduce draw calls by combining
compatible material regions where possible. The existing PART 35
triangle inspector + the existing material instance check (E8)
are the runtime-side helpers for staying within budget.

Avoid creating a separate material for every small object. Use
atlases and shared materials. PART 38.10's material hierarchy
(skin / cloth / leather / metal / bone / magic) is the right
granularity — one material per category, not one material per part.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.19 — IMPLEMENTATION ROADMAP (9 PHASES)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

If you are migrating a large existing character subject, walk the
phases in order. Each phase produces a reviewable intermediate.

  Phase 1  Improve the specification layer (PART 38.5).
  Phase 2  Build reusable continuous body templates (PART 38.6).
  Phase 3  Add deforming rig support (PART 38.12).
  Phase 4  Add clothing and armor layers (PART 38.10).
  Phase 5  Add UVs and texture generation (PART 38.11).
  Phase 6  Add baked animation (PART 38.13).
  Phase 7  Add GLB export and TypeScript loading (PART 38.14).
  Phase 8  Add automated review renders (PART 38.17).
  Phase 9  Add LOD and optimization (PART 38.18).

This roadmap is the long-form guide for reworking an existing
character subject. For a fresh character subject, the AI walks the
14-stage production pipeline from PART 38.4 instead — same stages,
different framing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.20 — PRIORITY ORDER (IF TIME IS LIMITED)
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

When you have limited time or limited vertex budget, implement
improvements in this order. Each step is a meaningful upgrade on
its own; the first 5 cover the diagnosis from PART 38.1.

  1.  Continuous head and torso topology
  2.  Real skeletal skinning (replaces transform-only animation
      on the deforming body)
  3.  Better facial proportions and inset eye sockets
  4.  Connected limbs with deformation edge loops
  5.  UVs and hand-painted texture variation
  6.  Fitted clothing and armor layers
  7.  Baked walk and attack animations
  8.  GLB export and TypeScript adapter
  9.  Multi-view validation
  10. LOD and compression

Do NOT start by adding more decorative primitive parts. That
increases complexity without solving the main quality problems.
The diagnosis from PART 38.1 is the one to keep re-reading until
it is no longer the description of your output.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.21 — FINAL DESIGN PRINCIPLE
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

A professional procedural character system is judged by the quality
of the silhouette, anatomy, face, deformation, materials, and
animation — not by the number of primitives or the raw vertex
count.

The target output is a character that:

  - reads clearly from every major angle (PART 38.17)
  - has continuous and intentional anatomy (PART 38.6)
  - deforms cleanly when animated (PART 38.12 - 38.13)
  - has a recognizable face and identity (PART 38.9)
  - uses believable material variation (PART 38.10 - 38.11)
  - keeps clothing and props physically attached (PART 38.10)
  - exports cleanly as GLB/GLTF (PART 38.14)
  - integrates cleanly into the TypeScript viewer (PART 1, PART 5,
    PART 6, PART 30, PART 33, PART 34, PART 35, PART 36, PART 37,
    this PART 38)
  - remains performant on desktop and mobile (PART 38.8, PART 38.18)

The central architectural change is to move from primitive assembly
to a continuous-topology, deformation-aware, GLB-first pipeline
while retaining TypeScript as the runtime integration and inspection
layer. PART 38 is the spec-side counterpart to that change.

================================================================================
PART 38 does NOT introduce:
  - A new mandatory field on any existing userData namespace
  - A new export path (no new factory signature; PART 1.2 still
    governs; the optional look-dev lights factory from PART 1.3
    still governs)
  - A change to the existing pivot-hierarchy approach from PART 30
    (which remains valid for non-character subjects per PART 38.2
    and for jointed-only character poses when the AI elects not to

  - A change to PART 34 (three-rig-helpers) — the E11 marker
    remains in force; the rig helpers are still the ONE sanctioned
    skeleton source when userData.rigGraph is declared
  - A change to the renderer's existing E1-E12 validators (E13-E16
    are NEW additions, not replacements; E1-E12 are unchanged)
  - A change to the GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE loaders
  - A change to the editor paste path
  - A change to the file-picker UI
  - A change to the scene tree or the Rig / Tris / Sockets buttons
  - A new dependency (no new import-map entry)
  - A change to the bundle format from PART 37
  - A change to the multi-file drop / model + rig pairing from
    PART 36

PART 38 layers on top of, never replaces:
  - PART 1 (TS output contract)
  - PART 2 (type safety)
  - PART 4 (imports)

Every existing rule still binds. PART 38 ADDS the character-pipeline

================================================================================
END OF PART 38 — v1.15 / v8.7 ADDITIONS: CHARACTER PIPELINE UPGRADE
The character output path is upgraded from primitive-assembly to
continuous-topology + deformation-aware + GLB-first, while
TypeScript remains the runtime integration and inspection layer.
The existing PART 30 pivot hierarchy + PART 34 three-rig-helpers
+ PART 35 triangle-first geometry + PART 36 multi-file drop +
PART 37 zip bundle support all continue to apply; PART 38 adds
the 14-stage production pipeline, the character specification
schema, the 4 continuous-mesh-generation approaches, the topology
+ edge-loop discipline, the polygon budget, the facial modeling
system, the clothing + armor + props system, the UV + texture
system, the real skeletal rigging standard, the animation
techniques, the GLB-first architecture, the tooling options, the
4 new validators (E13-E16), the multi-view review requirement,
the LOD strategy, the 9-phase implementation roadmap, the 10-step
priority order, and the final design principle. Nothing in this
PART removes or weakens any rule from PARTs 1-37.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------
38.22 — STYLE AGNOSTICISM: THE SYSTEM IS NOT LIMITED TO LOW-POLY
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

The project is named "Low Poly 3D" and the v1.0 → v8.6 history
favored low-poly output, but the renderer and the spec support
ANY 3D model style. Low-poly is the default for short prompts
because the project is named for it, NOT because the system
rejects other styles. PART 38.22 records this explicitly so
the AI does not self-limit when the user asks for something
that is not low-poly.

  Supported styles (each one is a valid output):

    - low-poly        (the default, e.g. "low poly goblin")
    - mid-poly        (more triangles, still faceted, e.g.
                       "mid-poly stylized knight")
    - high-poly       (dense meshes, e.g. "high-poly creature")
    - smooth-shaded   (computeVertexNormals, no flatShading,
                       e.g. "smooth-shaded sports car")
    - stylized        (art-directed, may be any vertex count
                       but with a deliberate visual style,
                       e.g. "stylized owl")
    - photoreal       (PBR materials, smooth shading, dense
                       meshes, e.g. "photoreal spaceship")
    - voxel           (cube-based, e.g. "voxel dragon")
    - hand-painted    (canvas-texture-driven, e.g. "hand-painted
                       fantasy character")
    - toon / cel      (PART 33.1[4] cel-toon shading)
    - retro-PSX       (PART 33.1[4] retro-psx shading)
    - hologram        (PART 33.1[4] hologram shading)
    - wireframe       (PART 33.1[4] as a final overlay)
    - any combination of the above

  How to detect the requested style (in priority order):

    1. Explicit style keyword in the user prompt
       ("low-poly", "mid-poly", "high-poly", "smooth",
        "realistic", "photoreal", "voxel", "stylized",
        "toon", "cel", "hand-painted", "PSX", "retro", etc.)
    2. Reference image — match the image's actual style
    3. Default → low-poly (because the project is named for it)

  How the spec's existing low-poly guidance applies when the
  style is NOT low-poly:

    - "PART 2 primitives"  — still applies, but the AI is free
                              to use higher segment counts and
                              denser meshes
    - "PART 3 materials"   — still applies; PBR / smooth
                              materials are encouraged for
                              non-low-poly styles
    - "PART 7 palette"     — still applies
    - "PART 8 textures"    — still applies; canvas-texture
                              hand-painting is a valid tool
                              for hand-painted and stylized
                              styles
    - "PART 12 checklist"  — still applies; the only items that
                              are low-poly-specific are the
                              "keep it simple" reminders, and
                              those should be IGNORED when the
                              user has explicitly asked for
                              high-poly / smooth / photoreal
    - "PART 30 pivot
       hierarchy"          — still applies; the choice between
                              pivot hierarchy and SkinnedMesh
                              is a rig decision, not a style
                              decision
    - "PART 31 character
       sculpt enrichment"  — still applies; the macro/meso/
                              micro hierarchy is style-agnostic
    - "PART 35 triangle
       soft cap"           — still applies; the 50k soft cap
                              is a budget, not a style limit.
                              A smooth-shaded car or a voxel
                              dragon may exceed it, in which
                              case the AI should follow
                              PART 35.2 (mergeGeometries) and
                              the per-geometry metrics
                              inspector before claiming the
                              model is ready

  What PART 38.22 does NOT change:

    - No new fields, no new exports, no new contract
    - No change to the existing PART 1-PART 37 rules
    - No change to the existing E1-E12 or E13-E16 validators
    - No change to the renderer's hard anti-pattern guards
      (those are style-agnostic — a NaN position is a NaN
      position regardless of whether the model is low-poly
      or photoreal)
    - The project name stays "Low Poly 3D" (it's a brand /
      project name, not a system limit)
    - The renderer's default scene lights stay the same
    - The renderer's default material is still MeshStandardMaterial
      with PBR roughness/metalness (which works for every style)

  What PART 38.22 explicitly CLARIFIES for the AI:

    - If the user prompt contains the word "low-poly" or "low
      poly", generate a low-poly model. The existing spec
      guidance for low-poly applies.
    - If the user prompt contains the words "realistic",
      "photoreal", "smooth", "smooth-shaded", or similar,
      generate a model in that style. The same PART 1 factory
      shape applies. The AI should use higher segment counts
      on primitives, smooth shading (flatShading = false on
      MeshStandardMaterial), and PBR materials with appropriate
      roughness / metalness.
    - If the user prompt contains the word "voxel", generate a
      voxel model using BoxGeometry primitives. The same PART
      1 factory shape applies. The renderer handles voxel
      models natively.
    - If the user prompt contains the word "stylized" without
      a specific style keyword, the AI has discretion. The
      default is low-poly unless the AI has a reason to choose
      otherwise.
    - If the user prompt is too vague to determine the style
      ("a fox", "a robot", "a goblin"), the default is low-poly
      because the project is named for it. The AI is NOT
      forbidden from choosing a different style when the
      subject strongly implies one ("a watch" → realistic,
      "a Minecraft zombie" → voxel), but it should default
      to low-poly when no signal is present.
    - If the reference image is clearly NOT low-poly, the
      output should match the image's style. The image
      analysis protocol (PART 17 in TS, PART 27 in JSON/JS)
      is the source of truth for what the model should look
      like, including its style.

  Cross-reference:

    - PART 33.1[4] already documents cel-toon, hologram, retro-psx
      shading modes — those are renderer-side opt-in features
      that work for any subject, any style
    - PART 34 (three-rig-helpers) is style-agnostic — skeleton
      and skin weights are valid for low-poly AND high-poly
      characters
    - PART 35's triangle inspector and mergeGeometries advice
      is style-agnostic
    - PART 38.0's central principle ("more polygons do not
      automatically create a better model") is style-agnostic
      — it warns against uniformly increasing segments, not
      against any particular style
    - PART 38.1's diagnosis of the primitive-assembly approach
      is also style-agnostic — a high-poly model assembled
      from unrelated primitives has the same problems

PART 38.22 is the explicit "the system is not limited to
low-poly" clarification. It does NOT introduce any new rule.
It just makes the existing scope of the system explicit so
the AI does not self-limit.

================================================================================
END OF PART 38.22 — STYLE AGNOSTICISM
The system supports any 3D model style — low-poly, mid-poly,
high-poly, smooth-shaded, stylized, photoreal, voxel,
hand-painted, toon, cel, retro-PSX, or any combination.
Low-poly is the default for short prompts because the project
is named for it, NOT because the system rejects other styles.
The renderer's anti-pattern guards (E1-E16) are style-
agnostic. The PART 1 factory contract is style-agnostic. The
GLB / OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE loaders are style-agnostic. If
the user wants realistic, generate realistic. If they want
voxel, generate voxel. If they want low-poly, generate
low-poly. The AI's job is to match the request, not to
over-correct toward the project name.
================================================================================


================================================================================
PART 39 — v1.16 / v8.8 ADDITIONS: 3D MODELING TECHNIQUES
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "3D MODELING TECHNIQUES — practical guide
for reducing bugs and making better models" (2026-09-04). The guide
captures six practical disciplines the AI must follow when generating
.ts factory geometry (or any JSON/JS blueprint that ultimately ends
up as a Three.js Group). PART 39 layers on top of — never replaces —
PART 1, PART 2, PART 3, PART 4, PART 30, PART 32, PART 34, PART 35,
PART 36, PART 37, PART 38.

  39.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    Three practical disciplines drive the rest of PART 39:

    1.  Catch the dumb stuff EARLY (39.1) — silent-comment-
        declaration bugs and floating-part gaps are caught at
        boot with a `validate*Model()` walk, not at runtime by
        the user staring at a screen.
    2.  Build a VISUAL dev loop (39.2) — runtime knobs,
        `renderer.info` logs, and on-demand PNG snapshots make
        proportion work 10x faster than recompile.
    3.  Use the RIGHT geometry primitive (39.3) — the
        primitive that makes curves, the primitive that makes
        round profiles, the primitive that blends smoothly,
        the primitive that repeats. Stack boxes to fake curves
        is always wrong.

    Everything else in PART 39 (39.4 proportions, 39.5
    procedural, 39.6 the three things) flows from these
    three.

  39.1  CATCH THE DUMB STUFF EARLY
  --------------------------------

  39.1.1  Ban the "comment eats code" pattern.

    The pattern

        // ------- section header
        // -------const COL = { ...

    silently comments out the `const COL = {` declaration
    because the second line starts with `//`. ESLint can't
    catch this directly, but a simple custom rule can.

    The fix the AI must adopt going forward: ALWAYS put a
    blank line between a `//` separator and the next
    declaration. Specifically, never start a non-`//` line
    that comes after a `//`-prefixed separator with another
    `//`.

    BAD (silently breaks the file):
        // ------- section header
        // -------const COL = { ... };

    GOOD (blank line, no leading `//` on the const line):
        // ------- section header
        // -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
        const COL = { ... };   // on its own line, no leading //

    This rule applies to every .ts factory the AI emits. It
    is style-agnostic; low-poly / high-poly / voxel / smooth
    / photoreal all suffer the same bug.

  39.1.2  Bake a boot-time `validate*Model()` function into
          every .ts factory.

    The factory MUST call a `validateModel(root, opts)`
    helper at the end of `create*Model()` and log warnings
    (do NOT throw — the model still loads) for:

    - gaps > maxGap (default 0.02) between adjacent parts
      sorted by their X-axis bounding box (the canonical
      "floating parts" detector);
    - model X dimension < 0.5 or > 20 (sanity range);
    - total mesh count > 500 (caller should consider
      InstancedMesh — see 39.3.4).

    Reference implementation (Appendix C; re-exported on
    `window.lblSpec.helpers.validateModel` — see 39.8):

        function validateModel(root, opts = {}) {
          const nodes = (root.userData.sculptRuntime as any)?.nodes ?? {};
          const ranges = Object.entries(nodes).map(([n, o]) => {
            const b = new THREE.Box3().setFromObject(o);
            return {
              name: n,
              minX: b.min.x, maxX: b.max.x,
              minY: b.min.y, maxY: b.max.y,
              minZ: b.min.z, maxZ: b.max.z
            };
          }).sort((a, b) => a.minX - b.minX);

          const errors: string[] = [];
          for (let i = 0; i < ranges.length - 1; i++) {
            const gap = ranges[i+1].minX - ranges[i].maxX;
            if (gap > (opts.maxGap ?? 0.02)) {
              errors.push(
                `Gap ${gap.toFixed(3)} between ` +
                `${ranges[i].name} and ${ranges[i+1].name}`
              );
            }
          }

          const total = new THREE.Box3().setFromObject(root);
          const size = total.getSize(new THREE.Vector3());
          if (size.x < 0.5) errors.push(
            `Model too small: total X = ${size.x.toFixed(3)}`);
          if (size.x > 20)  errors.push(
            `Model too large: total X = ${size.x.toFixed(3)}`);

          let meshCount = 0;
          root.traverse(o => { if (o.isMesh) meshCount++; });
          if (meshCount > 500) errors.push(
            `Too many meshes: ${meshCount} (consider InstancedMesh)`);

          return {
            ok: errors.length === 0,
            errors,
            meshCount,
            bbox: { size }
          };
        }

    The factory's tail should look like:

        export function create*Model(): THREE.Group {
          // ... build root ...
          const v = validateModel(root, { maxGap: 0.02 });
          if (!v.ok) {
            console.warn(
              `[create*Model] ${v.errors.length} issue(s):`,
              v.errors
            );
          }
          return root;
        }

    The renderer's E17 validator (see 39.7.1) re-runs the
    same walk on every load, so the user sees the warnings
    in the topbar chip even when the factory's own call is
    quiet.

  39.2  BUILD A VISUAL DEV LOOP
  -----------------------------

  39.2.1  Add runtime knobs (Leva / dat.gui / Tweakpane).

    Make scale, mag curve angle, stock taper, recoil
    strength — every dimension that the user can
    meaningfully tune — tweakable in real time. The AI finds
    proportions 10x faster by sliding a slider than by
    recompiling. Default the suggested knob to Tweakpane
    (already widely used; the Tweakpane `Pane` import is
    the recommended path for v1.16 / v8.8).

    Reference implementation (Appendix B; the `pane.addBinding`
    pattern is documented in full there):

        import { Pane } from 'tweakpane';
        const params = {
          scale: 1.8,
          magCurve: 0.22,
          stockTaper: 0.045,
          fireInterval: 0.10,
        };
        const pane = new Pane();
        pane.addBinding(params, 'scale', { min: 0.5, max: 3.0 })
            .on('change', e => { root.scale.setScalar(e.value); });
        pane.addBinding(params, 'magCurve', { min: 0.0, max: 0.5 })
            .on('change', e => { rebuildMagazine(e.value); });

    The factory does NOT need to ship the Pane code in the
    final emission — the Pane is for the AI's own
    authoring-time iteration. The factory should still
    publish the parameters as a top-level `params` object
    (see 39.5.2) so they are auditable in the final file.

  39.2.2  Log `WebGLRenderer.info` for sanity.

    Every .ts factory's `tick(dt)` (if present) and every
    dev-time harness should log:

        console.log(renderer.info.render);   // triangles, calls, geometries

    The signal "1 tris  1 calls" is suspicious — it usually
    means the model isn't actually rendering or `tick()` is
    not being driven. The renderer's E18 validator (see
    39.7.2) catches the "too many meshes" case; the
    `renderer.info` log is the author's own sanity check
    while iterating.

  39.2.3  Snapshot a frame to PNG on demand.

    Drop this helper (Appendix A; re-exported on
    `window.lblSpec.helpers.snapshotToPng` — see 39.8)
    behind a button or `?debug=1` query param:

        function snapshotToPng(renderer, path) {
          renderer.render(scene, camera);
          const c = renderer.domElement;
          const url = c.toDataURL('image/png');
          // save url to disk via fetch, or auto-download:
          const a = document.createElement('a');
          a.href = url;
          a.download = path || 'snapshot.png';
          a.click();
        }

    The AI iterates visually without leaving the browser.
    Useful while the AI is building the model — not
    required in the shipped .ts factory.

  39.3  USE THE RIGHT GEOMETRY PRIMITIVE
  --------------------------------------

    The "chain of rings" bug is a CLASS of error: stacking
    rotated boxes to fake a curve always reads as separate
    rings. The fix is not "more boxes with smaller gaps" —
    it is "use the primitive that makes curves". The full
    intent-to-primitive table:

      Want to make...             Use                              Don't use
      -------------------------  -------------------------------   -------------------------
      Curved surface (mag,       ExtrudeGeometry along a           Stacked rotated boxes
      grip, sling, sling-strap)  CatmullRomCurve3
      Round profile (barrel,     LatheGeometry                     32 stacked boxes
      cap, magazine tube)
      Smoothly blended shape     BufferGeometry with               Multiple overlapping
      (organic, melted)          computed vertices                 meshes
      Repeated detail (vents,    InstancedMesh                     50 individual meshes
      holes, rivets, buttons)

  39.3.1  Curved surface → ExtrudeGeometry along a
          CatmullRomCurve3.

    A banana mag, a curved handguard, a slung sling — all
    are extruded cross-sections along a curve, not
    rotated boxes. Appendix A's `curvedPart(curve, crossW,
    crossD, mat, opts)` helper (re-exported on
    `window.lblSpec.helpers.curvedPart` — see 39.8) is the
    canonical implementation. The `opts.steps` default
    of 80 is high enough for a smooth visual curve; lower
    it to 20 for a chunky low-poly curve.

  39.3.2  Round profile → LatheGeometry.

    A barrel, a bottle cap, a magazine tube, a bolt body
    — all are 2D profiles revolved around an axis. Use
    `new THREE.LatheGeometry(points, segments)`. The
    `segments` default of 12 is fine for low-poly; raise
    to 32 for smooth / photoreal.

  39.3.3  Smoothly blended shape → BufferGeometry with
          computed vertices.

    For a "melted" or organic shape, build the vertex
    array directly: `Float32Array` of XYZ triples, an
    index buffer for the triangulation, then
    `geometry.computeVertexNormals()`. Do NOT try to
    blend multiple overlapping meshes — the seams will
    show.

  39.3.4  Repeated detail → InstancedMesh.

    Vents, rivets, screw heads, buttons, repeated panels
    — all are one InstancedMesh with N matrix4
    transforms, NOT N individual meshes. The renderer's
    existing PART 35 already covers mergeGeometries for
    ONE-OFF detail; PART 39.3.4 covers REPEATED detail
    with InstancedMesh (the two compose cleanly).

  39.3.5  Useful rule of thumb.

    If you find yourself writing a `for` loop to make N
    copies of the same mesh along a path, STOP. Use
    ExtrudeGeometry or InstancedMesh instead. The loop
    version is always worse — worse performance, worse
    silhouette, worse authoring.

  39.4  PROPORTIONS & RECOGNITION
  -------------------------------

  39.4.1  The silhouette test.

    Render the model in pure black against white at
    256×256 and squint. If you can't tell what it is, the
    proportions are wrong — NOT the textures, NOT the
    lighting, NOT the details. The silhouette carries
    80 % of recognizability. The banana curve, the long
    barrel, the gas tube, the stock taper — these ARE the
    silhouette.

  39.4.2  Reference first.

    Keep a folder of 3-4 real reference photos and trace
    the outline before writing geometry. Use real-world
    ratios:
      - total length / barrel length / receiver length
      - mag curve radius (~250 mm on a real AK)
      - stock angle (~5° downward from bore axis)

    For the image-driven paths (Image_To_Ts.txt,
    Image_To_Js.txt, Image_To_Json.txt), the image-to-3D
    analysis protocol (PART 17 of the TS spec; PART 27 of
    the JSON/JS specs) IS the reference. The AI extracts
    proportions and landmark positions from the reference
    image and uses them to drive the part library and
    config object from 39.5.

  39.4.3  The "is it cheating" check.

    If a part of the model requires the user to be at
    exactly the right angle to look right, it's a hack.
    Real shapes look right from any angle. If a barrel
    only reads as a barrel at 90° to the camera, the AI
    has a silhouette problem, not a perspective problem.

  39.5  PROCEDURAL / PARAMETRIC MODELING
  -------------------------------------

  39.5.1  Build a part library.

    Every model is combinations of the same primitives:

        barrel(r, len)
        taperedBox(x0, x1, h0, h1, d)
        curvedMag(curve, w, d)
        woodBlock(x, y, z, w, h, d)

    Once the AI has these, building an AKM, an RPK, an
    SVD becomes an afternoon instead of a week. The
    Appendix A `curvedPart` helper is one member of the
    library; the others are factory-local. Every .ts
    factory should declare its part-library helpers
    BEFORE the `create*Model()` body.

  39.5.2  Drive everything from a config object.

    All colors, dimensions, curve points, recoil numbers
    — in a SINGLE config object at the top of the file
    (TS path: a `const CONFIG: *Config = { ... }`; JSON
    path: a top-level `params: { ... }` object on the
    blueprint root). Now the AI can have presets:

        const AK47_CONFIG: Ak47Config = {
          barrelLen: 0.66, magCurveAngle: 0.22, ...
        };
        const AKM_CONFIG:  Ak47Config = {
          barrelLen: 0.66, magCurveAngle: 0.22, ...   // same
        };
        const RPK_CONFIG:  Ak47Config = {
          barrelLen: 0.92, magCurveAngle: 0.15, ...   // longer barrel, straighter mag
        };

    The `params` object on the JSON/JS blueprint root is
    the analog. The renderer exposes it on the loaded
    scene root as `root.userData.params` so the user can
    tweak it at runtime via the Tweakpane panel (39.2.1).

  39.6  THE THREE THINGS THAT HELP RIGHT NOW
  ------------------------------------------

    1.  Snapshot to PNG on every save
          → fastest visual feedback loop
    2.  Run a bbox-overlap validator at boot
          → catches floating parts automatically
    3.  Stop stacking boxes to fake curves
          → use ExtrudeGeometry + CatmullRomCurve3 the
            first time

    Every other item in PART 39 follows from one of these
    three.

  39.7  RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
  ------------------------------------

    Two new non-blocking validators are added to the
    existing 16 (E1-E12 from PART 32, E13-E16 from
    PART 38) in `index.html`'s `ANTI_PATTERN_CHECKS` array
    (the same `lblSpec` object that already owns the rest
    of the spec runtime). Like all other PART 32 / 38
    validators, these are NON-BLOCKING: the model still
    loads, the user just sees a friendly toast + a red
    spec chip so they know the AI's generation deviated
    from the targeted spec.

  39.7.1  E17 — boot-time bbox-overlap gap detector
          (PART 39.1.2 / 39.6[2]).

    Walks the loaded model's `userData.sculptRuntime.nodes`
    map (TS path) or, for the JSON/JS path, every node in
    `blueprint.nodes[]`. Sorts entries by their X-axis
    bounding-box min. For each adjacent pair, computes the
    gap = next.minX - current.maxX. If gap > 0.02 (the
    PART 39.1.2 default `maxGap`), emits a warning naming
    both parts and the gap size.

    On the JSON/JS path the validator runs on the
    assembled scene, not the blueprint text (the
    blueprint text only carries the nodes' positions, not
    their world-space bboxes). The validator's tolerance
    is the same 0.02 default; a future v1.17 / v8.9 round
    can promote the tolerance to a config field if users
    ask for it.

  39.7.2  E18 — draw-call / mesh-count sanity
          (PART 39.6[3]).

    Counts the total number of THREE.Mesh nodes under
    the root. If meshCount > 500, emits a warning
    pointing at InstancedMesh (PART 39.3.4). This is the
    "draw-call / mesh-count" half of `renderer.info.render`
    (the triangles / vertices half is already in the
    renderer's PART 35 / 🔺 Tris panel — see index.html
    line 22350's `openTrianglePanel`).

  39.7.3  E17 / E18 do NOT change load behavior.

    Both validators are non-blocking. A model that emits
    E17 or E18 warnings still loads, still renders, is
    still exportable. The validators exist to surface
    authoring issues to the AI / maintainer, not to
    reject the user's scene.

  39.8  CANONICAL HELPERS (re-exported on window.lblSpec)
  --------------------------------------------------------

    Three new helpers join the existing PART 35
    `__threeTriHelpers` bundle (createUnifiedLimbSegment,
    createLowPolyKneeBlade, createIndexedPyramid,
    buildLowPolyTurretWithTris) on
    `window.lblSpec.helpers`:

    - `curvedPart(curve, crossW, crossD, mat, opts)`
        The Appendix A ExtrudeGeometry-along-curve
        helper. Build a curved mag, curved handguard,
        curved sling strap with one call:

            const magCurve = new THREE.CatmullRomCurve3([
              new THREE.Vector3(0, 0, 0),
              new THREE.Vector3(0, -0.05, 0.10),
              new THREE.Vector3(0, -0.12, 0.20),
              new THREE.Vector3(0, -0.18, 0.30)
            ]);
            const mag = lblSpec.helpers.curvedPart(
              magCurve, 0.082, 0.055, M.bakelite,
              { steps: 80, bevel: true }
            );

    - `validateModel(root, opts)`
        The Appendix C boot-time validator. Walks
        `root.userData.sculptRuntime.nodes`, reports
        gaps, mesh count, and bounding-box size. The
        factory's `create*Model()` tail calls this and
        logs warnings; the renderer's E17 validator
        (39.7.1) re-runs the same walk on every load.

    - `snapshotToPng(renderer, scene, camera, fileName)`
        The Appendix A PNG snapshot helper. Renders one
        frame, calls `renderer.domElement.toDataURL('image/png')`,
        triggers a download with the given file name. For
        AI authoring-time iteration; not required in the
        shipped .ts factory.

    These are pure additions to the existing
    `__threeTriHelpers` bundle — the existing 5 helpers
    stay untouched. The renderer's runtime contract
    update is `lblSpec.helpers.curvedPart`,
    `lblSpec.helpers.validateModel`, and
    `lblSpec.helpers.snapshotToPng` become the canonical
    way to do these three things in a .ts factory; the
    factory does NOT need to copy the function bodies.

  WHAT PART 39 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
  --------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing userData
      namespace (the existing `userData.sculptRuntime` is
      the consumer of `nodes` for E17; no new top-level
      userData key is required)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB / OBJ / STL /
      FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts paths are unchanged)
    - No new dependency (the new helpers use only
      Three.js APIs that are already imported; Tweakpane
      is the AI's authoring-time choice and is not
      required at runtime)
    - No new validator that blocks the model from
      loading (E17 and E18 are non-blocking, like E1-E16
      before them)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-38 rules (PART 39
      reinforces them — see the 39.0 cross-references)
    - No change to the existing E1-E16 validators (they
      stay exactly as they were in v1.15 / v8.7)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D" name
      (PART 38.22's style-agnosticism still applies —
      PART 39 is also style-agnostic)

  CROSS-REFERENCES
  -----------------

    PART 39 layers on top of, never replaces:
      - PART 1  (factory contract)        — TS path
      - PART 2  (primitives)              — TS path
      - PART 3  (materials)               — TS path
      - PART 4  (single-file rule)        — TS path
      - PART 17 (image-to-3D analysis)    — TS image path
      - PART 27 (image-to-3D analysis)    — JSON/JS image path
      - PART 30 (rigging & animation)     — both paths
      - PART 31 (character sculpt)        — character subjects
      - PART 32 (anti-pattern guards)     — E1-E12
      - PART 35 (triangle-first geometry) — E12, the 🔺 Tris panel
      - PART 36 (multi-file drop)         — both paths
      - PART 37 (zip bundle)              — both paths
      - PART 38 (character pipeline)      — E13-E16
      - PART 38.22 (style agnosticism)    — both paths
      - PART 65 (JSON/JS anti-patterns)   — E1-E12 on JSON/JS

================================================================================
END OF PART 39 — v1.16 / v8.8 ADDITIONS: 3D MODELING TECHNIQUES
Six disciplines for reducing bugs and making better models:
catch the dumb stuff early, build a visual dev loop, use the
right geometry primitive, drive proportions and recognition,
build a part library + config object, ship the three things
that help right now. Adds E17 bbox-overlap gap detector and
E18 draw-call / mesh-count sanity to the existing 16
non-blocking validators. Re-exports `curvedPart`,
`validateModel`, and `snapshotToPng` on `window.lblSpec.helpers`
alongside the PART 35 tris bundle. Style-agnostic, applies
to every .ts factory, every JSON/JS blueprint, every
subject. No new dependency, no new export path, no new
mandatory field, no removed / weakened rule.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 40 — v1.17 / v8.9 ADDITIONS: BETTER MODELS THROUGH BETTER GENERATION
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "System Update Details for Better TypeScript
Low-Poly 3D Models" (2026-08). The document distills 8 categories of
improvements the AI must apply when generating a .ts factory module
(or any JSON/JS blueprint that ultimately ends up as a Three.js
Group): (1) better prompt & input handling, (2) a multi-stage
generation pipeline, (3) mesh quality & topology improvements,
(4) structured TypeScript output improvements, (5) a full texture &
material system, (6) parametric & procedural techniques, (7) post-
processing, validation & feedback, and (8) scene / composition level
ideas. PART 40 layers on top of — never replaces — PART 1, PART 2,
PART 3, PART 4, PART 17, PART 27, PART 30, PART 31, PART 32, PART 34,
PART 35, PART 36, PART 37, PART 38, PART 38.22, PART 39, PART 64, and
PART 65 / PART 66.

  40.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    The 8 categories from the source doc are not 8 separate
    concerns — they are 8 facets of a single principle:

      Build a PRODUCTION-GRADE GENERATION PIPELINE, not a
      one-shot prompt-to-geometry call.

    A production-grade pipeline is one where:

      - The input (prompt + reference image) is structured
        (Subject + Material + Style + Technical Constraints,
        40.1) and preprocessed (background removal, contrast,
        centering, multi-view, 40.1.2-40.1.3) before any
        geometry work begins.
      - The geometry is generated in clearly separated STAGES
        (40.2) — coarse base mesh, geometry refinement,
        texture / material, final cleanup — so a mistake in
        one stage can be fixed without redoing the others.
      - The mesh that comes out of those stages is CLEAN
        (40.3) — watertight, no duplicate vertices, no
        non-manifold edges, no thin spikes, intelligent
        retopology, smart low-poly with a target triangle
        budget, feature-preserving simplification.
      - The TypeScript output is STRUCTURED (40.4) — modular
        factories, options-based geometry construction,
        metadata (poly count, style tags, generation
        settings), editable and maintainable.
      - The materials carry a FULL PBR TEXTURE SET (40.5) —
        base color, normal, roughness, metallic, AO — with
        style-specific texturing (flat low-poly colors vs
        painted vs realistic), separate texture refinement
        stage, and automatic UV handling where possible.
      - The geometry is PARAMETRIC & PROCEDURAL (40.6) —
        factory + options object, seed-based controlled
        variation, LOD support, prefer algorithmic geometry
        over pure black-box AI meshes.
      - The output is VALIDATED (40.7) — quality metrics
        (manifold check, poly count, symmetry, visual
        fidelity), multiple variants ranked, simple mesh
        repair pipeline before export, optional user rating
        feedback loop into prompt defaults.
      - (Optional) The result composes into a SCENE (40.8)
        with consistent style and density across multiple
        objects, hierarchical scene building, and procedural
        placement and variation.

    PART 40 reinforces PART 39 — the "build a part library +
    drive it from a config object" discipline from 39.5 IS
    the factory + options pattern from 40.4 + 40.6. The
    "use the right geometry primitive" discipline from 39.3
    IS the algorithmic-geometry-over-black-box rule from
    40.6. PART 40 is the production-engineering framing of
    the same ideas; PART 39 is the authoring-time framing.

    PART 40 is style-agnostic. The principles apply to
    low-poly, mid-poly, high-poly, smooth-shaded, stylized,
    photoreal, voxel, hand-painted, toon / cel, retro-PSX,
    hologram, wireframe, and any combination. Low-poly is
    the default for short prompts because the project is
    named for it (PART 38.22), not because PART 40 rejects
    other styles.

  40.1  BETTER PROMPT & INPUT HANDLING
  -----------------------------------

    The AI's first job is to turn the user's free-form text
    (and optional reference image) into a STRUCTURED brief
    before any geometry is authored. The brief is the input
    to the multi-stage pipeline (40.2), not the geometry
    itself.

  40.1.1  Structured prompt format.

    Decompose every prompt into 4 named slots:

      [Subject]    + [Material]    + [Style]    + [Technical Constraints]

    Examples:

      "a frog"          → S: frog, M: unspecified, St: unspecified, TC: unspecified
      "wooden crate"    → S: crate, M: oak wood,    St: low-poly,  TC: unspecified
      "AK-47, weathered steel, mid-poly, 20k tris" →
        S: AKM assault rifle, M: weathered steel, St: mid-poly, TC: ~20,000 tris
      "smooth-shaded ceramic teapot, 4k normal map, PBR, 1 unit tall" →
        S: teapot, M: glazed ceramic, St: smooth-shaded, TC: PBR + 4k normal + 1u height

    When the prompt is ambiguous (e.g. "a car" with no
    style), the AI picks the project default (low-poly)
    per PART 38.22 and records the choice in
    `meta.style` and `meta.styleDefaulted: true` so the
    user can see it was inferred, not declared.

  40.1.2  Image preprocessing.

    When a reference image is supplied, the AI should
    perform a small set of pre-processing steps before
    analyzing it:

      - Background removal (or at least background
        desaturate) so the silhouette is unambiguous.
      - Contrast / sharpness boost so the silhouette
        edges are clean.
      - Centering — translate the subject to the
        image center; the AI then knows the bbox is
        symmetric around the origin in screen space.
      - 256x256 black-on-white silhouette render for
        the PART 39.4.1 silhouette test.

    The image-to-TS analysis protocol (PART 17) and the
    image-to-JSON/JS analysis protocol (PART 27) both
    benefit from these steps. The AI does NOT need to
    bake the preprocessing into the shipped .ts factory
    — it's authoring-time only.

  40.1.3  Multi-view image support.

    When the user supplies 2-4 reference views (front,
    side, top, 3/4), the AI should:

      - Treat the FIRST image as the canonical silhouette
        source for the PART 39.4.1 silhouette test.
      - Use the SECOND image to validate the side
        proportions (the classic "is the barrel really
        that long?" check).
      - Use the THIRD image (top view) to validate the
        silhouette footprint — the PART 39.4.3 "is it
        cheating" check.
      - Use the FOURTH image (3/4 view) as the
        recognition / recognition test source — the
        angle the user will most likely see the model
        from in the renderer's orbit camera.

    On the JSON/JS path, the blueprint's `meta.referenceViews`
    field lists the view angles; on the TS path, the
    factory's `userData.sculptRuntime.referenceViews` does
    the same.

  40.1.4  Negative prompts and hard constraints.

    Negative prompts are constraints the AI must NOT
    violate. They are first-class inputs, not afterthoughts:

      - "no thin spikes" — no triangle with one edge
        more than 50x longer than another (the renderer's
        E20 hard-constraint check enforces this at
        runtime; see 40.7.2).
      - "watertight" — every edge is shared by exactly 2
        faces; the renderer's E20 + the AI's authoring-time
        manifold check enforce this.
      - "clean topology" — no duplicate vertices, no
        non-manifold edges, no holes; the AI's authoring-
        time cleanup + PART 39.1.2 boot-time validator
        enforce this.
      - "≤ 20k tris" — explicit target triangle budget;
        the renderer's E21 validator enforces this at
        runtime; the AI's authoring-time simplify step
        enforces it before export (see 40.3.2).

    The AI treats every negative prompt as a HARD
    constraint, not a soft preference. A model that
    violates a negative prompt is rejected, not
    "shipped with a warning".

  40.2  MULTI-STAGE GENERATION PIPELINE
  -------------------------------------

    A production-grade generation pipeline separates the
    work into 4 STAGES. Mistakes in one stage can be fixed
    without redoing the others.

      Stage 1 — Coarse base mesh (silhouette + proportions)
      Stage 2 — Geometry refinement
      Stage 3 — Texture / material generation
      Stage 4 — Final cleanup + export

  40.2.1  Stage 1 — Coarse base mesh.

    Goal: lock in the SILHOUETTE and the PROPORTIONS
    using the lowest possible triangle count (often
    < 200 tris for the whole model). The base mesh may
    look crude — it doesn't need textures, materials, or
    detail; it needs to be RECOGNIZABLE at 256x256
    black-on-white (PART 39.4.1 silhouette test).

    On the TS path: the factory's Stage 1 emission
    should consist of the part-library primitives
    (barrel / taperedBox / curvedMag / woodBlock from
    PART 39.5.1) placed at the config-object-driven
    positions (PART 39.5.2). The AI's authoring loop
    is: place primitive → render silhouette → adjust
    primitive's offset / scale → repeat.

    On the JSON/JS path: the blueprint's Stage 1
    emission consists of `nodes[]` entries with
    `geometry.kind: "box" | "cylinder" | "sphere" |
    "lathe" | "tube" | "extrude"`, no textures, no
    materials, default flat color.

  40.2.2  Stage 2 — Geometry refinement.

    Goal: add the SECONDARY forms and detail that make
    the model look finished, while staying under the
    declared target triangle budget (40.1.4 / 40.3.2).

    On the TS path: the factory's Stage 2 emission
    adds:

      - Edge bevels and corner softening
        (BoxGeometry → RoundedBoxGeometry or
        ExtrudeGeometry with bevel).
      - Curved parts via lblSpec.helpers.curvedPart
        (PART 39.3.1) for banana mags, sling straps,
        handguards.
      - Repeated detail via InstancedMesh
        (PART 39.3.4) for rivets, screws, panels.
      - One-off detail via BufferGeometry merge
        (PART 35.2) for non-repeating complex shapes.

    On the JSON/JS path: the blueprint's Stage 2
    emission adds `geometry.kind: "lathe"` for round
    profiles, `geometry.kind: "tube"` for curved
    surfaces, `geometry.kind: "instanced"` for
    repeated detail, and `geometry.merge: true` for
    non-repeating merged shapes.

  40.2.3  Stage 3 — Texture / material generation.

    Goal: apply a FULL PBR TEXTURE SET (40.5) with
    style-specific texturing (flat low-poly colors vs
    painted vs realistic). The AI does NOT have to
    paint every texture by hand — it can:

      - Use procedural noise for normal / roughness
        maps.
      - Use the reference image's albedo as a starting
        point, then desaturate / quantize to the
        target style.
      - Use a single solid color when the style is
        flat low-poly (no PBR maps needed).

    On the TS path: the factory's Stage 3 emission
    uses lblSpec.helpers.pbrMaterial (40.10) when
    style is realistic, and a single MeshStandardMaterial
    when style is flat low-poly.

    On the JSON/JS path: the blueprint's Stage 3
    emission sets `material.kind: "pbr"` with the
    full texture set, or `material.kind: "flat"`
    with a single color.

  40.2.4  Stage 4 — Final cleanup + export.

    Goal: run the mesh cleanup pipeline (40.7.3) and
    export. The cleanup pipeline runs BEFORE the export
    step, not after — a model that exports with
    non-manifold edges is a model that breaks in
    downstream tools.

    On the TS path: the factory's Stage 4 emission
    calls lblSpec.helpers.validateModel (PART 39.1.2 /
    39.8) and logs warnings; the model's
    userData.sculptRuntime.exportReady = true marks
    it as clean for the renderer.

    On the JSON/JS path: the blueprint's Stage 4
    emission sets `meta.exportReady: true` after
    the AI's authoring-time manifold / vertex-cleanup
    passes have run.

  40.3  MESH QUALITY & TOPOLOGY IMPROVEMENTS
  ------------------------------------------

    The mesh that comes out of Stage 2 must be CLEAN
    before Stage 3 textures are applied. A noisy mesh
    makes a noisy normal map; a non-manifold mesh
    makes a broken GLB export.

  40.3.1  Automatic mesh cleanup.

    The AI's authoring-time cleanup pass MUST run the
    following 4 operations, in this order:

      1. Weld duplicate vertices (vertices within
         ε = 1e-5 of each other become one).
      2. Remove zero-area triangles (cross product
         of two edge vectors < 1e-6; the renderer's
         E15 validator from PART 38.7 surfaces these
         as warnings, but the AI should remove them
         at authoring time, not ship them).
      3. Remove unused vertices (vertices not
         referenced by any triangle).
      4. Remove degenerate edges (edges shared by
         only one triangle — these are the
         non-manifold edges that break the
         watertight guarantee from 40.1.4).

    On the TS path, the factory can use
    `BufferGeometryUtils.mergeVertices` (imported
    from `three/examples/jsm/utils/BufferGeometryUtils.js`)
    for step 1. The AI does NOT have to ship the
    cleanup code in the final emission — it's
    authoring-time only.

  40.3.2  Smart low-poly mode with target triangle
          budgets.

    When the user declares a target triangle budget
    (e.g. "≤ 5,000 tris"), the AI must run a
    feature-preserving simplification step (QEM /
    quadric error metrics) that respects the budget
    while preserving:

      - The silhouette (the part of the model that
        contributes to the PART 39.4.1 silhouette
        test).
      - Hard edges (the part of the model where
        two faces meet at a sharp angle — the
        edge between the receiver and the magazine
        well, for example).
      - Curved surfaces (the part of the model
        where smooth shading is required).

    The renderer's E21 validator (see 40.7.2)
    checks the runtime triangle count against
    the declared target; the AI's authoring-time
    simplification step is what actually enforces
    the budget.

    On the TS path, the factory declares the budget
    via `userData.sculptRuntime.targetTris` or
    `meta.targetTris`. On the JSON/JS path, the
    blueprint declares it via `meta.targetTris`.

  40.3.3  Feature-preserving simplification.

    The simplification step from 40.3.2 uses a
    feature-preserving variant of QEM that
    weights vertex collapses by:

      - 1.0 for vertices on the silhouette.
      - 0.8 for vertices on hard edges.
      - 0.3 for vertices on curved surfaces.
      - 0.1 for vertices on flat surfaces.

    This is a guideline, not a hard rule — the
    exact weights depend on the model. The point
    is that the AI should not use a single weight
    for all collapses; that produces the "blobby"
    look that PART 38.1 diagnoses as a symptom of
    primitive-assembly.

  40.3.4  Force watertight / manifold meshes.

    The negative prompt from 40.1.4 ("watertight")
    is enforced at the AI's authoring time AND at
    the renderer's E20 runtime check. The
    authoring-time check is a real manifold test
    (every edge shared by exactly 2 faces, every
    vertex reachable from every other vertex
    through faces). The runtime check is a
    best-effort heuristic (no thin spikes, no
    zero-area triangles, no unused vertices)
    that the renderer's E20 validator (40.7.2)
    surfaces as a non-blocking warning.

  40.4  STRUCTURED TYPESCRIPT OUTPUT IMPROVEMENTS
  -----------------------------------------------

    The .ts factory (or JSON/JS blueprint) that
    comes out of the 4-stage pipeline should be
    STRUCTURED, not a flat hard-coded data dump.

  40.4.1  Modular factory pattern.

    Every part of the model should be its own
    named factory function:

      - `createBarrel(opts)` returns a Mesh.
      - `createReceiver(opts)` returns a Mesh.
      - `createMagazine(opts)` returns a Mesh.
      - `createStock(opts)` returns a Mesh.

    The top-level `create*Model()` then composes
    these factories into a Group. The factories
    take a SHARED OPTIONS OBJECT (40.4.2) so a
    single config change (PART 39.5.2) ripples
    through the whole model.

    On the JSON/JS path, the equivalent is
    each `nodes[]` entry having a `factory` field
    that names the part-library primitive it
    uses (PART 39.5.1).

  40.4.2  Parametric / options-based geometry.

    Every factory function takes a typed options
    object:

      interface Ak47Config {
        scale: number;
        magCurve: number;
        magCapacity: number;
        stockTaper: number;
        fireInterval: number;
        colors: { receiver: number; mag: number; stock: number };
      }

    A single config object drives ALL dimensions,
    colors, curve points, and recoil numbers. This
    is the same pattern as PART 39.5.2 — PART 40
    reinforces it as the canonical shape for every
    factory.

  40.4.3  Better hierarchy (parts, groups, sockets).

    The factory's top-level Group has 4 named
    children:

      - `parts`     — the actual geometry
                       (receiver, barrel, mag, stock).
      - `bones`     — the rig skeleton (PART 30/34).
      - `sockets`   — the 4 canonical Socket_*
                       attachment points (PART 33.1[7]).
      - `meta`      — metadata (40.4.4).

    The PART 30 pivot hierarchy stays valid; PART 40
    extends it with the 3 additional named children.

  40.4.4  Metadata (poly count, style tags,
          generation settings).

    Every .ts factory publishes a `meta` object
    on `userData.meta`:

      meta: {
        subject: "AKM assault rifle",
        style: "mid-poly",           // PART 38.22
        styleDefaulted: false,       // PART 40.1.1
        vertexBudget: 12000,         // PART 38.13
        targetTris: 5000,            // PART 40.3.2
        seed: 0xA1B2C3D4,            // PART 40.6.2
        referenceViews: ["front", "side", "top", "3/4"],
                                   // PART 40.1.3
        negativePrompts: ["no thin spikes", "watertight"],
                                   // PART 40.1.4
        generationPipeline: "v1.17", // which PART 40 was used
        exportReady: true            // PART 40.2.4
      }

    The renderer's E13 / E21 / E20 validators all
    read from this metadata. PART 40 introduces
    `targetTris`, `seed`, `referenceViews`,
    `negativePrompts`, `generationPipeline`, and
    `exportReady`; it does NOT change the existing
    `vertexBudget` / `style` / `subject` fields
    from PART 38.

  40.5  TEXTURE & MATERIAL SYSTEM
  -------------------------------

    The materials that come out of Stage 3 should
    carry a FULL PBR TEXTURE SET, not just a base
    color.

  40.5.1  Full PBR texture sets.

    A production-grade material has 5 maps:

      - baseColor    (sRGB)
      - normal       (linear, optional for flat styles)
      - roughness    (linear, 0=smooth, 1=rough)
      - metallic     (linear, 0=dielectric, 1=metal)
      - ao           (linear, ambient occlusion)

    On the TS path, the factory uses
    lblSpec.helpers.pbrMaterial (40.10) which
    returns a configured MeshStandardMaterial
    with all 5 maps wired up. The renderer's
    E19 PBR-completeness validator (40.7.2)
    checks that a model whose `meta.style`
    is "realistic" or "smooth-shaded" actually
    has all 5 maps; models whose style is
    "low-poly" or "voxel" are exempt (a single
    baseColor is sufficient for those styles).

  40.5.2  Style-specific texturing.

    The texture density and channel count
    depends on the style:

      - low-poly / voxel / hand-painted:
        baseColor only, often solid.
      - mid-poly / stylized / toon:
        baseColor + a small normal map for
        surface detail.
      - high-poly / smooth-shaded /
        photoreal / retro-PSX:
        full 5-map PBR set with high-resolution
        textures.

    The AI records the chosen texture density
    in `meta.textureDensity: "low" | "mid" | "high"`
    so the renderer's E19 validator can apply
    the right exemption rule.

  40.5.3  Separate texture refinement / re-texturing
          stage.

    A change to the texture set should NOT
    require redoing the geometry. PART 40
    reinforces the Stage 2 / Stage 3 split from
    40.2.2 / 40.2.3 — the geometry is done after
    Stage 2, and only the materials change in
    Stage 3.

    On the TS path, the factory's
    `setStyle(newStyle)` function re-applies the
    Stage 3 materials without touching Stage 2
    geometry. On the JSON/JS path, the
    blueprint's `meta.style` is a top-level
    field that the renderer's shading dropdown
    (PART 33.1[4]) already pivots on.

  40.5.4  Automatic UV handling.

    When a UV unwrap is required, the AI prefers
    the following 3 options, in order:

      1. Use the primitive's built-in UVs
         (BoxGeometry, CylinderGeometry,
         SphereGeometry all ship with valid UVs).
      2. Use a planar projection for flat
         surfaces (no unwrap needed).
      3. Use a simple box / cylinder unwrap
         (Three.js doesn't ship a generic UV
         unwrapper; the AI uses a hand-rolled
         planar-on-largest-face unwrap for
         custom BufferGeometry).

    The renderer's E19 UV-coverage validator
    (40.7.2) checks that UVs are within [0,1]
    and that there are no degenerate (0-area)
    UV islands.

  40.6  PARAMETRIC & PROCEDURAL TECHNIQUES
  ----------------------------------------

    Prefer algorithmic geometry over pure
    black-box AI meshes. The factory + options
    object pattern from PART 39.5 IS the
    parametric pattern from 40.6.

  40.6.1  Factory + options object.

    Already covered by 40.4.1 + 40.4.2. PART 40
    reinforces the pattern: every part of the
    model is a named factory that takes a
    shared options object.

  40.6.2  Seed-based controlled variation.

    When the model should have variation across
    instances (e.g. the AI is generating 3
    variants of a tree), the factory takes a
    `seed` option and uses a SEEDED RNG
    (lblSpec.helpers.seededRandom, 40.10)
    for every random decision (leaf rotation,
    branch length, bark color jitter). The
    same seed produces the same variant; a
    different seed produces a different
    variant. This is the production-grade
    alternative to "reroll and hope".

    The factory records the chosen seed in
    `meta.seed` (40.4.4) so the renderer can
    re-create the exact variant on reload.

  40.6.3  LOD (Level of Detail) support.

    The factory emits 3 LOD levels:

      - LOD0: full quality (10-20k vertices
        for characters, 5-8k for props).
      - LOD1: 50% of LOD0 (smart simplify
        from 40.3.2).
      - LOD2: 25% of LOD0 (aggressive simplify
        for far-away views).

    The THREE.LOD object (Three.js core) is
    the canonical container. The renderer's
    camera-distance-based LOD switching is
    NOT new in PART 40 — the LOD emission IS
    new. Models that don't have 3 LODs still
    load; the renderer just uses LOD0 for
    every distance.

  40.6.4  Prefer algorithmic geometry.

    When the user asks for "a forest" or
    "a city", the AI does NOT generate 200
    individual tree meshes or 200 individual
    building meshes. It generates 1 tree
    factory + 1 InstancedMesh (PART 39.3.4)
    of 200 instances, or 1 building factory
    + 1 InstancedMesh of 200 instances. The
    algorithmic-geometry approach is what
    keeps the model under the PART 35.2 50k
    triangle soft cap and the E18 500-mesh
    sanity check.

  40.7  POST-PROCESSING, VALIDATION & FEEDBACK
  --------------------------------------------

    The 4-stage pipeline (40.2) is followed by a
    post-processing step that catches what the
    earlier stages missed.

  40.7.1  Quality metrics.

    The AI's authoring-time validation runs
    4 metrics, in this order:

      1. Manifold check (40.3.4).
      2. Poly count vs target (40.3.2).
      3. Symmetry check (PART 38.16 — the
         model is bilaterally symmetric about
         its Y axis to within ±5%).
      4. Visual fidelity (PART 39.4 — the
         256x256 black-on-white silhouette
         matches the reference image's
         silhouette to within IoU ≥ 0.7).

    The renderer's runtime validators (E13,
    E15, E16, E19, E20, E21 — see 40.7.2)
    re-run the first 3 metrics on every load.
    The visual fidelity metric is authoring-
    time only.

  40.7.2  Renderer-side runtime validators
          (E19, E20, E21).

    PART 40 adds 3 new non-blocking validators
    to the existing 18 (E1-E18 from PART 32 /
    PART 38 / PART 39):

      - E19 — PBR + UV completeness
        (PART 40.5.1 / 40.5.4). For models
        whose `meta.style` is "realistic",
        "smooth-shaded", "stylized", or
        "photoreal", every material should
        have a baseColor AND at least one of
        (normalMap, roughnessMap, metalnessMap).
        Additionally, every geometry that has
        a UV-mapped material should have a UV
        attribute with values in [0, 1] and
        no degenerate (0-area) UV islands.
        Models with `meta.textureDensity: "low"`
        are exempt.

      - E20 — Hard-constraint compliance
        (PART 40.1.4 / 40.3.4). Walks every
        triangle; flags triangles where one
        edge is more than 50x longer than
        another (the "thin spike" detector).
        The renderer surfaces the first 4
        flagged triangles in the warning.
        This is a best-effort heuristic —
        true manifold detection requires a
        full topology rebuild — but it
        catches the obvious "this triangle
        will render as a single pixel"
        case.

      - E21 — Target triangle budget
        (PART 40.3.2). If the model declares
        `meta.targetTris` (and optionally
        `targetTrisTolerance`), the actual
        triangle count should not exceed
        target × (1 + tolerance). Default
        tolerance is 25% for low-poly and
        mid-poly subjects, 15% for high-poly
        and photoreal subjects (stricter).
        Non-blocking; the model still loads.

    E19, E20, E21 are non-blocking warnings,
    like E1-E18 before them. The model still
    loads; the user sees a toast + a red spec
    chip. They mirror the E1-E18 pattern and
    integrate into the existing
    `validate()` / `applyToUI()` / `revalidate()`
    pipeline without any caller-side changes.

  40.7.3  Mesh repair pipeline before export.

    The AI's authoring-time repair pipeline
    runs BEFORE the GLB / OBJ / STL export:

      1. Weld duplicate vertices (40.3.1[1]).
      2. Remove zero-area triangles
         (40.3.1[2]).
      3. Remove unused vertices (40.3.1[3]).
      4. Recompute normals if the cleanup
         pass changed the topology.
      5. Re-validate (40.7.1).

    A model that exports with non-manifold
    edges is a model that breaks in downstream
    tools (Blender, Unity, Unreal). PART 40
    requires the repair pipeline; PART 39
    recommended it; PART 38 (character
    pipeline) hinted at it via the GLB-first
    architecture. The pipeline is now a
    hard requirement for every .ts factory,
    every JSON/JS blueprint.

  40.7.4  Optional user rating feedback loop
          into prompt defaults.

    When the user rates a generated model
    (e.g. "this one looks good" / "this one
    is too noisy"), the rating feeds back
    into the AI's prompt defaults. The
    renderer does NOT have to implement the
    feedback loop UI in v1.17 / v8.9 — that's
    a future round. PART 40 documents the
    INTENT so the AI's authoring-time
    defaults can be tuned from the rating
    data when the UI ships.

    On the TS path, the factory records the
    last user rating in
    `userData.sculptRuntime.lastRating`. On
    the JSON/JS path, the blueprint records
    it in `meta.lastRating`. Both are
    optional.

  40.8  SCENE / COMPOSITION LEVEL IDEAS
  -------------------------------------

    (Optional expansion — applies when the
    model is one of N objects in a scene,
    not when it's the single root of the
    renderer.)

  40.8.1  Consistent style and density.

    When the AI is generating multiple
    objects for a single scene (e.g. "a
    medieval village"), every object should:

      - Use the same `meta.style` (low-poly,
        mid-poly, etc.).
      - Use the same `meta.textureDensity`
        (low, mid, high).
      - Use compatible LOD levels (one
        object at LOD2 next to another at
        LOD0 looks wrong).
      - Use the same material kind for
        similar surfaces (all wood is
        `material.kind: "wood"`, all metal
        is `material.kind: "metal"`).

    The AI records the scene-level
    conventions in a `sceneStyle` object
    on the root: `meta.sceneStyle = { style,
    textureDensity, lod, materialKinds }`.
    Every object in the scene inherits from
    it.

  40.8.2  Hierarchical scene building.

    A scene is a TREE of Groups, not a
    flat list of Meshes:

      Scene
      ├── Terrain (Group)
      │   ├── Ground (Mesh)
      │   ├── Hills (Group of 3 Meshes)
      │   └── Water (Mesh)
      ├── Buildings (Group of 4 InstancedMesh)
      ├── Props (Group of 12 InstancedMesh)
      └── Lighting (Group of 3 DirectionalLight + 1 AmbientLight)

    The PART 30 pivot hierarchy rules apply
    to every Group; PART 36 multi-file drop
    applies when the scene is shipped as a
    bundle; PART 37 zip bundle applies when
    the bundle is zipped.

  40.8.3  Procedural placement and variation.

    When the scene is procedural (e.g. "100
    trees scattered across a terrain"), the
    AI uses the seededRandom helper (40.6.2)
    with a scene-level seed. The same seed
    produces the same scene; a different
    seed produces a different one. The
    scene's `meta.seed` records the chosen
    seed so the renderer can re-create the
    exact scene on reload.

  40.9  RENDERER-SIDE RUNTIME CONTRACT
  ------------------------------------

    PART 40 adds 3 new non-blocking validators
    to the existing 18:

      - E19 — PBR + UV completeness
        (PART 40.7.2). For models whose
        `meta.style` is realistic / smooth-
        shaded / stylized / photoreal, every
        material should have a baseColor AND
        at least one of (normalMap,
        roughnessMap, metalnessMap), and
        every UV-mapped geometry should have
        a UV attribute with values in [0, 1]
        and no degenerate islands. Models
        with `meta.textureDensity: "low"`
        are exempt.
      - E20 — Hard-constraint compliance
        (PART 40.7.2). Walks every triangle;
        flags triangles where one edge is
        more than 50x longer than another
        (the "thin spike" detector). Surfaces
        the first 4 in the warning.
      - E21 — Target triangle budget
        (PART 40.7.2). If `meta.targetTris`
        is declared, the actual triangle count
        should not exceed target × (1 +
        tolerance). Default tolerance is 25%
        for low-poly / mid-poly, 15% for
        high-poly / photoreal.

    All 3 are non-blocking; the model still
    loads, the user sees a toast + a red
    spec chip. They mirror the E1-E18
    pattern and integrate into the existing
    `validate()` / `applyToUI()` / `revalidate()`
    pipeline without any caller-side changes.

    What PART 40 does NOT change in the
    renderer runtime:

      - No change to E1-E18 (they stay
        exactly as they were in v1.16 / v8.8).
      - No change to the GLB / OBJ / STL /
        FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts export
        pipeline.
      - No change to the import map.
      - No change to the loader (the loader
        still routes `.ts` / `.json` / `.js`
        / `.zip` files the same way).
      - No change to the file-picker UI.
      - No change to the scene tree or
        the Rig / Tris / Sockets buttons.
      - No new mandatory field on any
        existing userData namespace — the
        new fields from 40.4.4
        (targetTris, seed, referenceViews,
        negativePrompts, generationPipeline,
        exportReady, textureDensity,
        sceneStyle, lastRating) are ALL
        optional.
      - No new dependency.

  40.10 CANONICAL HELPERS
  -----------------------

    PART 40 adds 3 new canonical helpers to
    the existing `__threeTriHelpers` bundle
    (PART 35) and the existing PART 39 bundle
    (`curvedPart`, `validateModel`,
    `snapshotToPng`). The .ts factory can call
    any of them via
    `window.lblSpec.helpers.<name>` without
    copying the function body.

    - `pbrMaterial(opts)`
        PART 40.5.1 / 40.5.2. Build a
        MeshStandardMaterial with a full
        PBR texture set: baseColor (sRGB),
        normalMap (linear), roughnessMap
        (linear), metalnessMap (linear),
        aoMap (linear). The helper accepts
        either explicit map objects or
        `{ baseColor: '#hex', roughness:
        0.7, metalness: 0.0 }` shorthand.
        Returns a configured
        MeshStandardMaterial ready to
        attach to a Mesh. Falls back to
        a single-color MeshStandardMaterial
        when no maps are provided, so a
        flat low-poly style still works.

    - `seededRandom(seed)`
        PART 40.6.2 / 40.8.3. A Mulberry32-
        based seeded RNG that returns
        `{ next(): number in [0,1),
        range(min, max): number,
        int(min, max): integer,
        pick(arr): element,
        bool(p): boolean }`. The same seed
        always produces the same sequence;
        a different seed produces a
        different one. Used for procedural
        variation, scene placement, and
        noise-driven material jitter.

    - `triCountSummary(root)`
        PART 40.7.2. Returns a quick
        triangle count summary: total
        triangles, total meshes, top 5
        meshes by triangle count, and
        the per-style budget check from
        E21 (if `meta.targetTris` is
        declared). The renderer's E21
        validator uses the same function,
        so the factory's authoring-time
        call and the renderer's runtime
        call see the same number.

    These are pure additions to the
    existing `__threeTriHelpers` bundle —
    the existing 5 PART 35 helpers and the
    3 PART 39 helpers stay untouched. The
    renderer's runtime contract update is
    `lblSpec.helpers.pbrMaterial`,
    `lblSpec.helpers.seededRandom`, and
    `lblSpec.helpers.triCountSummary`
    become the canonical way to do these
    three things in a .ts factory; the
    factory does NOT need to copy the
    function bodies.

  WHAT PART 40 DOES NOT INTRODUCE
  --------------------------------

    - No new mandatory field on any existing
      userData namespace (the new fields in
      40.4.4 — targetTris, seed, referenceViews,
      negativePrompts, generationPipeline,
      exportReady, textureDensity, sceneStyle,
      lastRating — are ALL optional; the
      current renderer ignores unknown keys
      safely)
    - No new export path (the existing GLB /
      OBJ / STL / FBX / DAE / USDZ / .ts paths
      are unchanged)
    - No new dependency (the new helpers use
      only Three.js APIs that are already
      imported; no import-map change)
    - No new validator that blocks the model
      from loading (E19, E20, E21 are non-
      blocking, like E1-E18 before them)
    - No change to the existing PART 1-39
      rules (PART 40 reinforces them — see
      the 40.0 cross-references)
    - No change to the existing E1-E18
      validators (they stay exactly as they
      were in v1.16 / v8.8)
    - No change to PART 38.22's style
      agnosticism (PART 40 is also style-
      agnostic — applies to every subject,
      every style, every path; low-poly is
      the default for short prompts because
      the project is named for it, not
      because PART 40 rejects other styles)
    - No change to PART 39.5's factory +
      options pattern (PART 40 reinforces
      it as 40.4.1 + 40.4.2 + 40.6.1)
    - No change to the project's "Low Poly 3D"
      name

  CROSS-REFERENCES
  -----------------

    PART 40 layers on top of, never replaces:
      - PART 1  (factory contract)        — TS path
      - PART 2  (primitives)              — TS path
      - PART 3  (materials)               — TS path
      - PART 4  (single-file rule)        — TS path
      - PART 17 (image-to-3D analysis)    — TS image path
      - PART 27 (image-to-3D analysis)    — JSON/JS image path
      - PART 30 (rigging & animation)     — both paths
      - PART 31 (character sculpt)        — character subjects
      - PART 32 (anti-pattern guards)     — E1-E12
      - PART 34 (rig helpers)             — pre-bundled helpers
      - PART 35 (triangle-first geometry) — E12, the 🔺 Tris panel
      - PART 36 (multi-file drop)         — both paths
      - PART 37 (zip bundle)              — both paths
      - PART 38 (character pipeline)      — E13-E16
      - PART 38.22 (style agnosticism)    — both paths
      - PART 39 (3D modeling techniques)  — E17-E18
      - PART 39.5 (factory + options)     — both paths
      - PART 64 (character sculpt enrich) — JSON/JS only
      - PART 65 (JSON/JS anti-patterns)   — E1-E12 on JSON/JS
      - PART 66 (architectural bridge)    — JSON/JS only

    Primary repositories to study for the
    techniques in PART 40 (the AI should
    skim these repos when authoring-time
    inspiration is needed, but does NOT
    need to import from them):
      - https://github.com/jasonsturges/three-low-poly
        (parametric factory + options pattern)
      - https://github.com/VladimirGl/Code3DBench
        (code generation quality evaluation)
      - https://github.com/vinhhien112/Three.js-Object-Sculptor-Codex-Plugin
        (structured ObjectSculptSpec + TypeScript factory)
      - https://github.com/AndrewSink/3D-Low-Poly-Generator
        (mesh decimation and low-poly conversion)
      - https://github.com/dgreenheck/ez-tree
        (parametric generation with many tunable parameters + LODs)
      - https://github.com/syl-p/procedural-generator-lowpoly-map
        (TypeScript procedural low-poly generation)
      - https://github.com/troisjs/little-planet
        (procedural low-poly planet — style consistency)
      - https://github.com/dreamlong1/LowPolyCity
        (full procedural low-poly city — scene composition)

================================================================================
END OF PART 40 — v1.17 / v8.9 ADDITIONS: BETTER MODELS THROUGH BETTER GENERATION
A production-grade generation pipeline: better prompts + multi-stage
workflow + mesh quality + structured TS + PBR materials + parametric
techniques + validation + scene composition. Adds E19 PBR + UV
completeness, E20 hard-constraint compliance (thin spike detector),
and E21 target triangle budget to the existing 18 non-blocking
validators. Re-exports `pbrMaterial`, `seededRandom`, and
`triCountSummary` on `window.lblSpec.helpers` alongside the PART 35
tris bundle and the PART 39 curvedPart / validateModel / snapshotToPng
bundle. Style-agnostic, applies to every .ts factory, every JSON/JS
blueprint, every subject. No new dependency, no new export path, no
new mandatory field, no removed / weakened rule.
================================================================================

================================================================================
PART 41 — v1.18 / v8.10 ADDITIONS: HIGH-DETAIL GENERATION
================================================================================
Source: the maintainer's "System High-Detail Update Plan" (2026-08).
The plan targets a specific gap: current models ship in the ~20 KB
range, while higher-end systems (img2threejs and similar) ship in
the >60 KB range. The gap is not about geometry size — it's about
GEOMETRIC DENSITY, the count of secondary and tertiary forms, and
the discipline of authoring detail BEFORE writing code. PART 41
adds the rules, validators, and helpers that close this gap.

PART 41 layers on top of — never replaces — PART 1, PART 2, PART 3,
PART 4, PART 17, PART 27, PART 30, PART 31, PART 32, PART 34, PART 35,
PART 36, PART 37, PART 38, PART 38.22, PART 39, PART 40, PART 64,
PART 65, and PART 66. PART 41 reinforces PART 40's production-grade
pipeline framing with a stronger density-first discipline and a
5-stage pipeline (PART 40's 4 stages become the first 4 of PART 41's
5, with Stage 4 "Surface Detail" promoted from a Stage 2 sub-bullet
to its own stage).

  41.0  CENTRAL PRINCIPLE
  -----------------------

    ONE rule drives the rest of PART 41:

      PREFER GEOMETRIC DENSITY OVER CODE BREVITY.

    The current models stay low-detail because of three
    anti-patterns the AI has fallen into:

      1. Single-pass generation — the AI tries to ship
         a complete factory in one shot, which caps the
         component count at "what fits in one mental
         pass". PART 41 splits the work into 5 STAGES
         (41.2) so each stage can focus on one concern.
      2. Overly DRY / compact code style — the AI tries
         to minimize line count, which minimizes
         secondary and tertiary forms. PART 41 inverts
         this: when in doubt, add a layer. The
         `prefer many small forms over few large ones`
         rule (41.3.3) is the explicit instruction.
      3. No detail inventory before code — the AI
         jumps into geometry without listing what
         identity / secondary / tertiary details the
         subject MUST have. PART 41 requires a
         DETAIL INVENTORY (41.1) before any geometry
         is written, and the inventory becomes the
         factory's checklist.

    The 7 PROMPT RULES (41.8) capture the same idea as
    7 sentences the AI can paste into its own system
    prompt. They are the "I will" half of the contract;
    41.1-41.7 are the "I will" half spelled out in
    detail.

    PART 41 is style-agnostic. The density rules
    apply to low-poly, mid-poly, high-poly,
    smooth-shaded, stylized, photoreal, voxel,
    hand-painted, toon / cel, retro-PSX, hologram,
    wireframe, and any combination. A dense low-poly
    model is still low-poly (it's the per-form triangle
    count that stays low); a dense photoreal model is
    still photoreal (it just has more PBR detail
    layers). PART 41's density rules do not change
    the style axis from PART 38.22; they add a
    DENSITY axis on top of it.

  41.1  DETAIL INVENTORY (before writing any geometry)
  ---------------------------------------------------

    Before the AI writes a single line of geometry, it
    MUST produce a DETAIL INVENTORY. The inventory is a
    structured list of what the model needs to ship with,
    broken into 4 categories.

      1. Identity-defining details (what makes it a
         knight, an AKM, a teapot, a tree).
      2. Secondary forms (the layers, plates, and
         props that frame the identity).
      3. Tertiary micro-details (rivets, seams,
         damage, folds).
      4. Material zones (which surfaces get which
         material family).

    The inventory is published on the model's metadata
    so the renderer can read it and the AI can audit
    it.

    On the TS path, the factory publishes the inventory
    on `userData.meta.detailInventory`:
